#5837. The Top 10 Reasons to Be Thankful in 2026

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: The Top 10 Reasons to Be Thankful in 2026

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

When the world is looking especially bleak—as it was after Election Day in 2024—making a list of the things I’m thankful for is a challenge. But that isn’t the case this year. Although the state of the world in 2026 contains much to regret and many things to worry about, there are some salient developments that have me feeling especially grateful as I contemplate the upcoming holiday. Without further ado, here are the top 10 things that I’m thankful for this year.

1. The U.S. Midterms. It turns out U.S. democracy is healthier than many people thought—which is not to say that it doesn’t need some serious improvements—and the Trump administration was as inept at rigging things as many of us had hoped. And make no mistake: This election was far more than the usual setback that incumbent parties face in the midterms. It was a drubbing of historic proportions. It took real political ineptitude to turn Texas and South Carolina blue, for example, and to produce a nine-seat swing in the Senate. Full points to the successful Democratic candidates and to party stalwarts like Stacey Abrams who laid much of the groundwork, but you have to give Trump and his minions credit for enacting policies that a clear majority of Americans really hated. Because I like the rule of law, the separation of powers, a competent and noncorrupt government, disciplined policy-making, separation of church and state, and other quaint traditions, I’m grateful that most of my fellow citizens agree (along with a dislike for foolish wars, cockeyed trade policies, predatory hegemony, oligarchic excess, sexual misconduct, rising living costs, and deliberate and probably illegal efforts to gut public services).

When the world is looking especially bleak—as it was after Election Day in 2024—making a list of the things I’m thankful for is a challenge. But that isn’t the case this year. Although the state of the world in 2026 contains much to regret and many things to worry about, there are some salient developments that have me feeling especially grateful as I contemplate the upcoming holiday. Without further ado, here are the top 10 things that I’m thankful for this year.

1. The U.S. Midterms. It turns out U.S. democracy is healthier than many people thought—which is not to say that it doesn’t need some serious improvements—and the Trump administration was as inept at rigging things as many of us had hoped. And make no mistake: This election was far more than the usual setback that incumbent parties face in the midterms. It was a drubbing of historic proportions. It took real political ineptitude to turn Texas and South Carolina blue, for example, and to produce a nine-seat swing in the Senate. Full points to the successful Democratic candidates and to party stalwarts like Stacey Abrams who laid much of the groundwork, but you have to give Trump and his minions credit for enacting policies that a clear majority of Americans really hated. Because I like the rule of law, the separation of powers, a competent and noncorrupt government, disciplined policy-making, separation of church and state, and other quaint traditions, I’m grateful that most of my fellow citizens agree (along with a dislike for foolish wars, cockeyed trade policies, predatory hegemony, oligarchic excess, sexual misconduct, rising living costs, and deliberate and probably illegal efforts to gut public services).

2. The Global Backlash Against Autocracy. Here’s something more to celebrate: This trend wasn’t just confined to the United States. I’m also thankful that voters in Brazil, Hungary, and Serbia made similar choices, rejecting would-be (or in some cases, incumbent) autocrats and their self-dealing schemes. To be sure, strongman rule continues in many places, and there is reason to worry about the upcoming election in France, the enduring popularity of right-wing parties in Germany, and the authoritarian trends that have returned to parts of Latin America. But I’ll give thanks that what looked like a cresting autocratic wave has been halted, at least for now.

3. Being a Jerk Lost Its Political Appeal. There is one final aspect of the political landscape that deserves a separate mention: The apparent rejection of asshole-ry in the body politic and a renewed desire for principled but civil behavior by political leaders. In recent decades, acting like a complete jerk—lying, engaging in vulgar name-calling, embracing misogyny and racism, trolling relentlessly, and such—had been a surprisingly effective political tactic. U.S. President Donald Trump is the leading example, of course, but a similar approach was embraced by the likes of Pete Hegseth, Vivek Ramaswamy, Laura Loomer, Ken Paxton, Lauren Boebert, Nancy Mace, and Greg Abbott, among others. It was part of the standard playbook of Fox News and provocateurs like Ben Shapiro. By 2026, in fact, acting like a jerk had become a central part of the MAGA/GOP brand. This approach was successful in part because it made Republicans look and sound like real fighters (as when Trump told a rally crowd that “I am your vengeance”) at a moment when mainstream Democrats seemed unclear about what they believed and terminally hamstrung by wokeness.

But over the past year, Democrats produced a bunch of candidates who made it clear that they had strong beliefs that they were willing to stand up for, even if their views were controversial. But at the same time, people like Zohran Mamdani, James Talarico, Abdul el-Sayed, Annie Andrews, and Jon Ossoff come across as likeable folks who want to focus on policies rather than personalities, and they don’t engage in a lot of nasty personal attacks. Notably, Democrats dumped toxic candidates such as Graham Plattner in Maine, while Republicans (and especially Trump) doubled down on some of theirs. Surprise, surprise: It turned out that American voters have grown tired (if not disgusted) by political figures are chronic liars, selfish, corrupt, and generally mean-spirited jerks, especially when they are also incompetent. One may therefore hope for the return of civility to American public discourse, which would help heal some of the country’s current divisions. And as one watches the British electorate sour on faux-populist Nigel Farage, one may even hope that this trend extends beyond the United States.

4. Balancing Behavior. I ’ll confess to a certain vested interest here, but I’ve been pleasantly struck by evidence of balancing behavior in a wide variety of contexts. Balance-of-power politics often gets a bad rap, as a world of competing powers is seen as one that exacerbates tensions and eventually leads to greater conflict. But as we saw during the unipolar era, unchecked power can be equally (if not more) dangerous and result in a bunch of pointless wars. One of the many problems with “liberal hegemony” is that it made global security overly dependent on U.S. engagements almost everywhere, which made peace dependent on Washington showing good judgment and consistently doing the right thing. Even before the arrival of Trump, there were good reasons to question that assumption.

If states balance readily against potential threats, and if leaders recognize that this is the case, then states that might be tempted to challenge the status quo will be deterred by the prospect of facing combined opposition. The knowledge that others will also face similar constraints also reduces every state’s need to make gains to be secure. Thus, efficient balancing behavior can be a powerful cause of peace, as it was during the Cold War.

For this reason, I’m heartened by the evidence of renewed balancing, even if it is currently less efficient than one might wish. Europe is rearming after years of neglect, Middle Eastern states are forging new arrangements that are less dependent on unilateral U.S. protection, and Asian countries continue to coordinate responses to rising Chinese power and assertiveness. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney’s call for joint efforts to counter the Trump administration’s predatory policies is consistent with this logic, as well. Let us hope that the next U.S. administration recognizes the danger of provoking ever-growing resistance, rediscovers the value of constructive ties with allies, and adjusts its behavior accordingly.

5. The Gulf on Israel Widens and Deepens. Last year, I expressed some thanks that U.S. public attitudes toward Israel were shifting, and that trend has accelerated over the past year. The electoral victories by candidates who were openly critical of Israel’s behavior is an obvious sign of this development, as is Senator Chris Van Hollen’s challenge to Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer’s efforts to become majority leader next year. Van Hollen may fall short, but the fact that he could pose as a credible alternative is evidence that we are living in a very different world.

My thanks that the American body politic is shifting to a more defensible position is tempered by my fear that it is too little and much too late. Had this shift occurred a couple of decades ago, it might have salvaged the two-state solution, marginalized Hamas politically, bolstered liberal forces within Israel and halted its own spiral toward extremism, opened the door to gradual détente with Iran, and left the Middle East far better off than it is today. While I’m thankful that the United States is finally coming to its senses on this issue, I cannot look upon the current situation with any sense of pleasure.

6. Tech Bros Brought Down to Earth. A year ago, tech bros like Elon Musk, Mark Zuckerberg, Sam Altman, Peter Theil, Jensen Huang, and others were riding high. Not only were they amassing unreal amounts of wealth, but they were pouring millions of dollars into the political system and performing end runs around efforts to rein them in.

I don’t want to overstate this trend—the latest developments at Nvidia underscore that tech leaders still have the ear of the president—but I’m glad to see public opinion and public officials begin to demand greater accountability from these self-designated masters of the universe. There’s a growing pushback against data centers, Musk’s millions didn’t prevent the blue tsunami, the saga of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang has also exposed potentially corrupt dealings by U.S. officials and revealed that some of the corporate titans care more about their own bank accounts than they do about national security. Looking ahead, it seems likely that politicians in the United States, Europe, and elsewhere are going to start regulating artificial intelligence. It’s about time.

Yes, I know: Huang has been reinstated at Nvidia, SpaceX and Anthropic had record-setting IPOs, and all these companies will likely play an outsized role in the economy and in politics for years to come. But for now, I’ll be thankful that some of their hubris has been checked. Heck, even Musk is now trying to deny that he was ever MAGA—despite the abundant evidence to the contrary. Either he’s lying or he has no principles at all—there’s no third option.

7. Dogs That Didn’t Bark. As I said at the outset, there’s no shortage of things to worry about if you’re looking for reasons not to feel thankful. Efforts to reduce climate change are failing; global public health issues are rising; the wars in Ukraine, Sudan, the Persian Gulf, etc., are still raging and causing vast suffering; measles is back in the United States (give thanks to the Trump administration and especially U.S. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. for that dubious “achievement”); and we are all wondering where the AI revolution will take us. If you want to have a gloomy Thanksgiving, therefore, that option is available.

Nonetheless, we should also pause and reflect on the bad things that could have happened but didn’t. The world economy didn’t suffer a new financial panic or depression. We didn’t face another global pandemic. Humanity marked the 81st consecutive year since Hiroshima in which nuclear weapons were not used and none of the great powers fought each other. The European Union didn’t collapse, the United States didn’t invade Greenland, China didn’t blockade or attack Taiwan, and the war in the Persian Gulf didn’t escalate in truly catastrophic fashion (e.g. large-scale attacks on desalinization plants).

8. Guilty (and Not So Guilty) Pleasures. As usual, I’m grateful for the people who educated, amused, inspired, and entertained me over the years. I enjoyed nonfiction works by Eli Clifton and Ian Lustick, Stephen Kinzer, Chad Levinson, Kimmo Rentola, and Daniel Walker Howe, as well the commentaries by David French, Paul Krugman, Lydia Polgreen, David Rothkopf, and most of the regular columnists at the Financial Times. I don’t get to read as many novels as I’d like, but Colson Whitehead, Alessandra Ranelli, Curtis Sittenfeld, Barbara Trapido, and a few others brought me a lot of enjoyment this past year. I never grow tired of rereading Raymond Chandler or Rex Stout, and I finally got around to reading Ursula K. Le Guin’s The Left Hand of Darkness, a mere 57 years after it was first published. My wife and I tapped our feet to big band jazz at Birdland, enjoyed Don Cheadle and Ayo Edebiri in Proof, and were blown away by Paul Giamatti’s performance in Eugène Ionesco’s Rhinoceros at the American Repertory Theater. The Red Sox resurgence fell short in the playoffs but their record-setting winning streak was great fun. The Yankees didn’t make the World Series, and this lifelong Dodgers fan found himself rooting for the Brewers instead. (I mean, seriously, how could one not?) And given my earlier predictions, it’s hard not to feel a bit smug about the demise of the Saudi-sponsored LIV Golf tour. I don’t watch much TV apart from sports, but I’m thankful they brought Ted Lasso back for one more season (but really, enough is enough), and I hope Apple TV renews Sugar for a third season.

9. The Golden Age of Academia. This item is bittersweet, as the world in which I have spent almost all my career is slowly vanishing. I feel enormous gratitude for having been part of it, yet I am filled with apprehension and sadness at its present trajectory. I’ve been a full-time academic for the better part of 50 years, and whatever success I have enjoyed is only partly due to my own efforts. In addition to a whole lot of lucky breaks, I was fortunate to be part of a golden era in American higher education, when society placed a high value on research and teaching, professors were generally respected, and both state and federal officials understood that a well-funded, unconstrained, and diverse academic community made the whole country stronger and wealthier.

Sadly, that world is now imperiled. A small part of the problem lies within higher education itself (excessive bureaucratization, overemphasis on intercollegiate sports, self-imposed limits on free inquiry, reluctance to police genuine misconduct, etc.), and the emergence of AI is almost certain to transform the entire enterprise in ways that are still hard to predict. But most of the trouble arises from a concerted attack on universities from the political right, a campaign that began long before Trump but has been supercharged under him. In addition to defunding key lines of research and damaging institutions once universally acknowledged as the world’s best (and a magnet for talented young people from all over the world), government efforts to impose ideological conformity on universities guarantees that the brightest minds will pursue other careers, important questions will receive inadequate attention, truth will get censored, and society will know less and misunderstand more. I remain deeply thankful for the life that I’ve been privileged to lead, and I’m alarmed by the obstacles that current and future scholars will face.

10. A Personal Note. I’ll spend a fair bit of the next three weeks reviewing the page proofs for a new book, which means I can spend Thanksgiving Day giving thanks that the damn thing is finished. The title is The Five Keys to a Safer World: A Foreign Policy Primer, and it will be published by Princeton University in late spring 2027. I’m immensely grateful to the institutions and individuals who made it possible (you can read about them in the acknowledgements), but I’m also greatly relieved that I’m not still writing it. I hope each of you can look back on some important achievement or milestone in 2026 and look forward to 2027 with anticipation and hope.

Stephen M. Walt is a columnist at Foreign Policy and the Robert and Renée Belfer professor of international relations at Harvard University. Walt is the author of The New Geopolitics, a five-part newsletter masterclass breaking down the biggest concepts you should use to navigate today’s world. #5835. How Europe Comes Apart

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MUJTABA RAHMAN is Managing Director for Europe at Eurasia Group.

The Four Elections That Could Seal the Continent’s Fate

The European Union is entering a period of political instability that could threaten its future—not through its formal dissolution, but through the gradual erosion of its capacity to act. In recent months, Europe has been engulfed in protests driven by a common crisis of affordability, deteriorating public services, and declining confidence in governments’ ability to improve living standards, especially for young people.

The protests that have swept across France, Germany, Italy, and Spain are not a single, coordinated European movement, but they reflect underlying problems that are increasingly common to a growing number of EU countries: a mounting perception that governments are unable to deliver affordable housing, functioning public services, and economic security. This widening gap between the public’s expectations and the economic and fiscal reality confronting many EU countries is only likely to grow over time. It could prompt Spanish voters to unseat Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez this weekend and usher in a right-wing government that includes the far-right party Vox. And it sets up 2027 to be a strikingly consequential year, as nine elections across the EU could completely remake the continent’s complexion.

Four of those elections, in France, Germany, Italy, and Poland, will be particularly important. In each of them, far-right populist forces could rise to the fore and turn their countries against greater EU integration, support for Ukraine, and collective European security. But dangers remain even if the far right does not win outright at the polls. A plethora of electoral outcomes could still see new governments pull away from Brussels and vitiate attempts to strengthen European autonomy and defense. Instead of a bang, the European project could die with a whimper.

The most consequential race will be in France. A series of domestic and international developments, along with a volatile national mood, have combined to make Marine Le Pen the front-runner to win the April 2027 election and become France’s first far-right president since the dissolution of the Vichy regime in 1944.

In October, riots and looting marred a series of mostly peaceful protests against high prices and deteriorating schools in a score of French cities. Those images—along with the diffuse sense of a country in paralysis—have shredded whatever remained of the domestic credibility of President Emmanuel Macron and his minority centrist coalition. Édouard Philippe, the strongest centrist contender to succeed Macron, has been tarnished by association. The first poll since the riots, conducted by IFOP for Le Figaro, showed Le Pen’s support among potential voters rising to 36 percent, her highest level to date, while support for Philippe fell three points from a month earlier to 15 percent.

Philippe and the other centrist challenger, Gabriel Attal, another Macron-era prime minister, were already losing support because of high energy prices caused by the wars in Iran and Ukraine. Both candidates remain stuck between seven percent and 15 percent in the polls, and their campaigns have generated little momentum or popular enthusiasm.

Meanwhile, an opportunistic coalition of far-left extremists and young looters from the poor multiracial suburbs of French cities who were responsible for much of the violence in October will hurt the far-left candidate Jean-Luc Mélenchon, who has been accused by the government of helping incite the unrest. He has certainly failed to condemn the disturbances. But the disorder has not dampened his allure, as support for his candidacy has risen to between 17 and 19 percent in polling, which makes him a credible contender to reach the second-round runoff against Le Pen. Unfortunately for Mélenchon, polls suggest that Le Pen would thump him in the second round, by at least 65 percent to 35 percent.

The election seems very much Le Pen’s to lose. No presidential candidate since the Fifth Republic began in 1958 has held such a commanding first-round lead in opinion polls five months before election day and lost.

A Le Pen presidency would be nothing short of an earthquake for Europe. It would deliver a death blow to the EU’s ambition of greater “strategic autonomy”: Brussels’ goal of achieving greater political independence from the United States and China, primarily via more European integration. This was a vision espoused by Macron in a speech at the Sorbonne in September 2017, in which he called for a “sovereign, united, and democratic Europe.” Le Pen is committed to unilaterally (and illegally) withholding French contributions to the EU budget; to ending French and EU aid to Ukraine; to restraining all EU spending; to blocking the enlargement of the EU to neighboring countries; and changing the French constitution to enshrine “national” primacy in jobs and welfare, which would effectively destroy the European single market. She may be denied some of these ambitions, but a hostile Paris could very easily stymie any forward movement of the EU.

Centrist governments are on the defensive beyond France, as voters across the continent conclude that they do not have the solutions to address their problems. Germany is a case in point. A weak and unpopular chancellor, Friedrich Merz, is finding it difficult to hold off the rise of the far-right, neo-Nazi Alternative for Germany party, known as the AfD, which continues to make sweeping electoral gains across the country.

Merz has to steward an economy that is struggling after decades serving as Europe’s industrial core. Although it is true that Germany’s economic outlook in 2026 and 2027 has brightened after almost flat growth in 2025, its recovery is driven exclusively by the government’s debt-financed investment programs. Key industries, including those to do with automobiles, machinery, pharmaceuticals, and chemicals, are still suffering from high energy prices, crumbling infrastructure, and a shrinking workforce.

China’s aggressive trade practices, deliberately aimed at undermining some of Germany’s core sectors, continue to put pressure on German companies. The unwinding of the international trade system also disproportionately hurts Germany’s export-driven economy. So does Europe’s fragmented and undercapitalized banking sector, which provides too little venture capital for German start-ups to scale up quickly. For these reasons, Berlin will have a hard time limiting significant job losses in its industrial sectors in 2027.

The result will be unemployment and growing electoral disgruntlement that Merz’s government will be poorly placed to address. If anything, the divides between the chancellor’s conservative camp and his coalition partner, the center-left Social Democrats, will prevent the government from delivering decisive reforms related to pensions, taxes, health care, the labor market, energy, and climate.

Five state elections in 2027, two of which are in the key states of North Rhine–Westphalia and Lower Saxony, will exacerbate this divide. Merz’s Christian Democrats will be vying for seats with the Social Democrats, keeping the two sides in a permanent competition that could prevent the coalition from governing effectively at the federal level. And that competition will likely be in vain: state election results for both parties will almost certainly be disappointing, leading to growing restiveness and speculation about Merz’s future as chancellor.

These centrifugal forces within the government will collide with the determination of both parties to stay in the coalition and avoid provoking a fresh election. This will result in a largely paralyzed government, discredited centrist politics, and a widening sense of malaise across an already unhappy and disaffected electorate.

As the center struggles, the AfD on the far right and Die Linke on the hard left will continue to gather national momentum. For the first time, the AfD will almost certainly penetrate deeply into the electorates of Germany’s western industrial strongholds in state elections next year, hollowing out the constituencies of both Christian Democrats and Social Democrats. With its sights on the 2029 federal elections, the AfD will increasingly look like a national contender rather than a protest movement rooted in eastern Germany. Die Linke, for its part, will continue to siphon off votes from left-leaning parties with its platform of economic grievance, disinterest in support for Ukraine, and the highly successful mobilization of young voters around support for Palestinians. The gains of both parties will further splinter state parliaments and make the process of government formation at that level increasingly cumbersome. The resulting state administrations will likely be unstable and doomed to performing poorly, deepening voter disillusionment with party politics and encouraging a greater willingness to experiment with radicalism.

These electoral pressures in Germany and in France make it likely that the relationship between Berlin and Paris will grow strained. A nationalist-populist president Le Pen would have little interest in sustaining the French-German “engine”—the traditional motor of European integration. Le Pen’s political priority of cutting French payments to the EU would rankle Germany, the largest contributor to the EU budget. And any government in Berlin would be unlikely to accommodate French positions, especially those of a disruptive Le Pen presidency, such as weakening the single market or abandoning Ukraine.

Without convergence between French and German policy, it will be harder for the EU’s other states to build consensus on the economic and security challenges that matter most. This prospect has encouraged European policymakers to look east, toward Poland, and ask what “new Europe” can do if “old Europe” ceases to drive the EU forward. Yet Warsaw faces its own political troubles, and it will struggle to galvanize the continent if France and Germany fall away.

Poland will stage consequential parliamentary elections next year. The vote could bring the overthrow of the current centrist coalition and the creation of a far-right coalition government involving the right-wing Law and Justice (PiS) party, the far-right Confederation Liberty and Independence, and the far-right Confederation of the Polish Crown (KKP).

A coalition formed by all three would mark a significant shift in Poland’s domestic and foreign policy. Such a government would adopt a more confrontational approach toward the EU, particularly on the issues of migration, climate policy, judicial independence, and the next seven-year EU budget. Although PiS remains broadly supportive of NATO and Ukraine, Confederation and especially the KKP have adopted a more critical stance toward Kyiv. Their participation in the government could therefore erode Poland’s support for Ukraine, including its military and financial assistance to its embattled neighbor, and lead to tougher policies regarding Ukrainian refugees, nearly one million of whom remain in Poland after fleeing Russia’s full-scale invasion in 2022, according to Eurostat.

Significant ideological differences among the three parties, particularly over economic policy, would probably make this far-right coalition unstable. PiS’s preference for social spending and state intervention sits uneasily alongside Confederation’s economically liberal agenda. Moreover, the leader of PiS, Jaroslaw Kaczynski, has repeatedly ruled out cooperation with KKP’s leader, Grzegorz Braun, describing him as “very dangerous” and his party as “completely unserious.” A far-right government could be quite short-lived, if it emerges at all.

An alternative but equally problematic scenario would involve a hung parliament in which neither Prime Minister Donald Tusk’s Civic Coalition (KO) nor PiS could assemble a governing majority, resulting in a weak minority government or even fresh elections. Fragmentation among smaller parties and disagreements over potential coalition partners could prolong negotiations, particularly if PiS refuses to cooperate with KKP while Tusk’s coalition lacks sufficient support from centrist and left-wing parties. Even if a minority government were formed, its dependence on opposition parties for legislative support could severely constrain policymaking, leaving Poland unable to address its mounting security challenges or play any leadership role in Brussels.

Italy, meanwhile, presents a different risk. Unlike France, Germany, or Poland, it currently has a relatively stable government led by a prime minister, Giorgi Meloni, who has reconciled her far-right nationalist origins with a broadly pragmatic approach to European and foreign affairs. Indeed, Meloni has become something of a poster child of far-right moderation, held up as evidence that ultranationalists can become responsible European leaders. But that equilibrium could be upset by the emergence of a new political force even further to Meloni’s right.

Meloni remains the favorite to win next year’s election. But she is threatened by Roberto Vannacci, a former paratrooper and veteran of the Iraq and Afghanistan wars who has launched a new party, National Future (FN), that is peeling away right-wing voters from Meloni with a nationalist, populist agenda that is skeptical of NATO and support for Ukraine. His goal is to court Italians aggrieved by Meloni’s compromises with Brussels.

The worst-case scenario would be a right-wing majority in which FN holds the balance of power and chooses to use it. That outcome would require the party to outperform expectations, approaching or exceeding ten percent of the vote—FN is currently polling at 6.5 percent—while Meloni’s Brothers of Italy party slips to around 20 percent or below. The coalition would still govern, but its center of gravity would shift decisively to the right, and Meloni would no longer have a firm grip at the helm.

Vannacci would likely not moderate his positions once in government, as Meloni did. Instead, he would maximize his leverage, up to and including threatening to pull out of the coalition and trigger snap elections, to extract concessions regarding Italy’s relations to Europe. His strategy would be deliberately confrontational: squeezing Meloni between the demands of his right-wing base and the constraints imposed by the EU, NATO, and the bond markets. The aim would be less to shape policy than to build his own visibility and lure supporters away from Meloni’s Brothers of Italy. Faced with a direct challenge on her right flank, Meloni would be forced to prioritize her own political survival, pulling the government toward more unorthodox positions.

The effects would be most acute in foreign policy, as Italy moved from the consensual position it has adopted on Ukraine to more active opposition, a major challenge at a time when EU decision-making on Ukraine, defense, and the EU budget depends heavily on unanimity among the EU’s largest members. Italy could transform from a pragmatic, generally constructive player in Brussels into a more disruptive force, blocking or threatening to veto decisions on bilateral and EU-wide support for Ukraine. Alongside a far-right Paris, Rome would also block Ukraine’s path to EU accession. On EU financial matters, Italy would straddle Germany and France, siding with Berlin in opposing any increase in the bloc’s next budget and with Paris in resisting cuts to agriculture and funding for the EU’s poorest regions, narrowing the space for a deal on the EU’s next seven-year budget and certainly curtailing its geopolitical ambitions. Under pressure from Vannacci, Meloni could also be forced to abandon NATO’s target for member states to spend five percent of GDP on their militaries and even forgo her planned incremental increases in military spending.

The government would push hard for more flexibility on EU fiscal targets and, in a more extreme scenario, breach the rules outright, reviving bond market volatility. On migration, Italy could refuse to take back asylum seekers from other member states under the EU’s Dublin rules, undermining implementation of the bloc’s migration pact.

In considering these elections together, it becomes clear that the danger facing the EU is not that the union will suddenly collapse but that the political consensus that underpins European integration could begin to unravel in its key member states. The risks are not all equal. A Le Pen presidency is the most likely and most consequential for the EU, while political paralysis in Germany also looks increasingly difficult to avoid. Although Poland’s elections could produce a moderate center-right government, a far-right government or a period of protracted instability now looks increasingly plausible. Italy’s trajectory is perhaps the least threatening, but a government dependent on Vannacci would create another impediment to broader European cooperation.

Nor do all four races need to deliver the worst-case scenarios for the consequences in the EU to be far-reaching. A Le Pen victory in France combined with paralysis in Germany would itself throw up major problems for the EU, including a possible bond market crisis in France that could infect the broader eurozone. If Poland and Italy also moved in that direction, the EU could find itself without a functioning coalition of major states willing and able to address the growing security and economic challenges the continent is facing.

The biggest victim of these looming changes is likely Ukraine and, by extension, Europe’s own security. The burden of supporting Ukraine financially would fall on a smaller group of member states in northwestern Europe, but they by themselves would not be able to fully compensate for the loss of support from the likes of France and Germany.

The EU has survived previous internal and external crises because its major powers ultimately judged that their interests were better served by collaboration and integration than by going their own way. The danger in 2027 is that this election supercycle will deliver outcomes that mean this assumption no longer holds. Even if the EU perseveres through the upheaval ahead, it could lose something much more profound: the underlying political consensus that has allowed the bloc to function since its creation, seven decades ago. That would not be the formal end of the European project. But it could mark the beginning of its irreversible decline.

How Europe Can Take More Responsibility—Without Starting an Arms Race

#5834. Politics, money and Thanksgiving dinner: Just two words can help avoid argument

Dr. Joel Brown, a communication expert, shares how replacing one common phrase and asking a simple question can help defuse disagreements and keep holiday gatherings civil.

As Americans across the country sit down to Thanksgiving dinner just weeks after Democrats' midterm sweep drastically reshaped the political landscape going into 2027, conversations about politics and the economy could turn tense.

Dr. Joel Davis Brown, a leadership expert, executive coach and author who studies how people communicate and navigate conflict, says swapping out the word "but" when discussing controversial topics can help keep the discussions civil.

"‘But’ shows disagreement," Brown told Fox News Digital. "It’s a way of telling the person that what they said should not be heard, and it really stifles conversation."

Instead, Brown suggests trying "yes, and." That doesn’t mean you have to agree, he said. Rather, it signals that you’ve heard the other person and leaves room to share a different view. "‘Yes, I’ve heard you and I have a different opinion,’" Brown said. "We can listen to varying opinions."

FOOD PRICES ARE RISING BEFORE THANKSGIVING. AN ECONOMIST SHARES WAYS TO SAVE

Dr. Joel Brown says the holiday dinner table can quickly become a source of tension when family members disagree, but choosing the right words can make all the difference in preventing heated exchanges. (iStock)

If a hot-button topic comes up, Brown recommends asking the person to "tell me more about that."

The question, he says, can help you understand how someone arrived at their view instead of turning the exchange into a debate.

He also cautions against opening with "I disagree," which can put people "in an oppositional place" before they have fully heard one another. Rather than trading facts and figures to win an argument, he said, people may make more headway by sharing personal stories and listening to the experiences behind someone else’s opinion.

"If you’re going into these exchanges as looking to win an argument, maybe change your approach," Brown said. "Maybe instead of trying to win that argument, you try to understand the other person’s perspective."

BLACK FRIDAY DEALS ARE EVERYWHERE. HERE’S HOW TO SPOT REAL SAVINGS

Holiday gatherings can bring family and friends together, but differing opinions and heated conversations can sometimes lead to tension. (iStock)

Brown said it helps to decide in advance what you want from the interaction, whether that’s getting through the meal, building a relationship or persuading someone. That goal can shape how you respond.

Political topics aren't the only ones that can get tense when families come together.

For personal questions about marriage, children or other life choices, Brown recommends setting expectations before the gathering. Talk with family or friends about what would help everyone feel comfortable, and be clear about what you do and do not want to discuss.

Additionally, if a conversation becomes stressful, take a breath, sip water or step away from the table. Brown said people should know their own triggers and remember they are not responsible for managing every interaction.

Making small changes to the way people communicate may help keep holiday celebrations peaceful, enjoyable and focused on togetherness. (iStock)

He encourages approaching disagreements with empathy, which he described as listening to another person’s story without taking on their position.

Family members may not see eye to eye, he said, but the holiday can still be a chance to learn from one another.

"Look at these as learning opportunities," Brown said.

Amanda Macias covers the intersection of business, economics and politics, with a focus on how policy decisions shape markets, businesses and American workers.

President Donald Trump said Rep. Ilhan Omar could be deported or face jail when asked about questions surrounding her age, calling the Minnesota Democrat "a disgrace to our country."

In a lengthy Thanksgiving message on Truth Social Thursday, President Donald Trump praised a small fraction of the Somali community, saying they should be allowed to remain in the United States, while calling for the immediate removal of those receiving government assistance while unemployed.

The holiday post mixed praise for "AMERICAN PATRIOTS" with criticism of the "Radical Far Left" and Minnesota’s Somali community, which Trump has frequently accused of public assistance fraud in the state.

"These are wonderful American ‘citizens,’ and should be allowed to stay in our Country, and celebrate Thanksgiving with us. The others should be thrown out, immediately, thereby saving the Great State of Minnesota, which has no chance if they are allowed to stay."

A demonstrator waves the flag of Somalia as a vehicle passes by a rally in protest against Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE), amid a reported federal immigration operation targeting the Somali community, in Minneapolis, Minnesota, on December 8, 2025. (Tim Evans/Reuters)

Trump’s remarks echo ongoing criticism from some Republicans regarding public benefit usage and fraud cases within the community.

"They are mostly gone now, brought back to their point of origin and, hopefully, living a beautiful and idyllic life, and promising, ideally, never to kill innocent people again," he wrote.

"We won’t have to worry about that, because they are GONE — but, Happy Thanksgiving to them anyway, even though from Faraway Lands," he added. "We miss you dearly, but you will never be back, and America will never allow what you have done to us to happen again!"

#5830. Universities, schools nationwide use Thanksgiving as chance to promote 'Day of Mourning' and decolonization

Loudoun County parent Rachel Pisani joins 'Fox & Friends' to express outrage over school lesson plans teaching children that Thanksgiving is a 'Day of Mourning.' Pisani argues the 'woke' curriculum teaches children to hate their country.

Thanksgiving is a day of celebration and spending time with family for the vast majority of Americans, but for several students on college campuses across the country, the national holiday is a source of anguish that is being expressed in the form of protests and counter-programming events.

On Nov. 18, George Washington University hosted an event called "Breaking Bread: The Cost of Celebration" that was described as a chance for students to examine Thanksgiving, Christmas and Independence Day through an intersectional lens, including experiences of "belonging, exclusion, cultural erasure, and historical harm."

"Through facilitated dialogue and shared reflection, students will engage in meaningful conversations about historical memory, social responsibility, and the complexities of celebrating within a diverse society," the student life event description read.

At the University of Wisconsin at Milwaukee, the American Indian Student Center & Electa Quinney Institute for American Indian Education hosted a Nov. 17 event called "AlterNative Giving," which was described as "a community gathering that offers a meaningful alternative to the traditional Thanksgiving narrative. Instead of focusing on colonial histories, we come together to honor Indigenous ways of knowing, being, and giving."

TRUMP DEI CRACKDOWN ‘MISSES CORE IDEOLOGY' AND MUST TARGET LINGERING DANGER ON CAMPUSES, WATCHDOG WARNS

A modern recreation of the first Thanksgiving in the autumn of 1621 at Plimoth Patuxet Museums (formerly Plimoth Plantation) in Plymouth, Mass. (Courtesy of Kathy Tarantola/Plimoth Patuxet Museums)

Salem State University promoted an event that labeled Thanksgiving as a "National Day of Mourning" and told students the land they are being educated on was stolen from Indigenous people.

"The land occupied by Salem State University is part of the traditional and ancestral homeland of the Massachusett tribe," the school’s website posted. "We acknowledge the genocide and forced removal of the Massachusett tribe and their kin from their summer home in Nâmkek."

CALIFORNIA COLLEGE LECTURER REMOVED AFTER ERUPTING AT STUDENT OVER QUESTION ON NATIVE AMERICANS

A review of university calendars found several examples lamenting the downsides of Thanksgiving, including Occidental College, which said the holiday is a "reminder of settler colonialism and its devastating impact on Indigenous individuals, communities and tribes."

The Pratt Institute lists "National Day of Mourning" as an occasion for Indigenous remembrance and reflection, and the University at Albany lists the holiday as "National Day of Mourning/Thanksgiving."

George Washington University hosted an event on Nov. 18 called "Breaking Bread: The Cost of Celebration." (iStock)

The colonial messaging around Thanksgiving isn't limited to universities but is also occurring at elementary and high schools, including in Loudoun County, Virginia, where a parent recently pushed back in an interview with Fox News about school lesson plans teaching children that Thanksgiving is a "Day of Mourning."

On its website, the San Diego County Office of Education explained that "November is Native American Heritage Month, a time to honor the diversity, history, and living cultures of Native peoples. It is also the month when Thanksgiving is observed in the United States, a holiday that carries very different meanings for different people, including many Native communities."

Oregon’s Bethel School District links to a "Decolonizing Thanksgiving: A Toolkit for Combatting Racism in Schools" on its official Equity, Access & Inclusion webpage. Listed under teaching resources, the toolkit appears alongside material about honoring Native peoples on Thanksgiving.

#5826. FLASHBACK: Two presidents pulled off secret Thanksgiving surprises for US troops — see the moments

Melania Trump delivers a powerful message of gratitude to military families during a visit to Fort Rucker ahead of Thanksgiving. She honors the profound sacrifices made by military spouses and children while their loved ones are deployed across the globe. She expresses deep appreciation for their strength and resilience in keeping their homes full of love.

Under a cloak of secrecy, two American presidents made surprise Thanksgiving visits to war zones, bringing a taste of home to U.S. troops serving thousands of miles from their families.

Sixteen years apart, President George W. Bush celebrated with American troops in Iraq in 2003, while President Donald Trump served Thanksgiving dinner to service members in Afghanistan in 2019. Both visits were carried out under tight secrecy, catching troops by surprise as they spent the holiday far from home.

Trump secretly traveled to Bagram Airfield in Afghanistan on Nov. 28, 2019, where he served turkey, posed for photos and thanked American troops for their sacrifices amid the ongoing war.

INSIDE TRUMP’S INTIMATE MEETING WITH NATION’S BRAVEST SOLDIERS DURING WHIRLWIND TEXAS TRIP

President Donald Trump serves Thanksgiving dinner to US troops at Bagram Air Field during a surprise visit on November 28, 2019 in Afghanistan. (OLIVIER DOULIERY/AFP via Getty Images)

"There's nowhere I'd rather celebrate this Thanksgiving than right here with the toughest, strongest, best, and bravest warriors on the face of the Earth," Trump told the troops at the time, later adding, "And you don't even know how much the people of our country love and respect you."

The visit came as roughly 12,000 American troops remained in Afghanistan more than 18 years after the U.S. invasion.

ARMY VET LAWMAKER MARKS ABBEY GATE ANNIVERSARY WITH BLISTERING MESSAGE FOR BIDEN-HARRIS: 'ABJECT FAILURE'

President Donald Trump serves Thanksgiving dinner to US troops at Bagram Air Field during a surprise visit on November 28, 2019 in Afghanistan. (OLIVIER DOULIERY/AFP via Getty Images)

During the visit, Trump met with Afghan President Ashraf Ghani to discuss efforts to end the war and told American troops that the Taliban wanted to reach a deal with the United States. He said his administration was reducing America's military presence in Afghanistan while pursuing a political solution to the more than 18-year conflict.

Less than two years later, President Joe Biden oversaw the chaotic U.S. withdrawal from Afghanistan in August 2021.

WOUNDED MARINES RELIVE THE HARROWING KABUL AIRPORT ATTACK IN EXPLOSIVE NEW FOX NATION DOCUMENTARY

Trump serves Thanksgiving dinner to US troops at Bagram Air Field during a surprise visit on November 28, 2019 in Afghanistan (OLIVIER DOULIERY/AFP via Getty Images)

The withdrawal unfolded as the Taliban rapidly seized control of Afghanistan, culminating in the fall of Kabul and a frantic evacuation at the city's airport.

Sixteen years earlier, Bush pulled off a similar Thanksgiving surprise in Iraq , secretly flying into Baghdad International Airport on Nov. 27, 2003, to celebrate with American troops just eight months after launching the U.S.-led invasion that toppled Saddam Hussein's regime.

GEORGE BUSH WELCOMES TOP OFFICIALS TO HIS HOME STATE FOR 9/11 REMEMBRANCE ON 25TH ANNIVERSARY OF ATTACKS

President George W. Bush helps serve Thanksgiving dinner at the Bob Hope Dining Facility, Baghdad International Airport, Iraq, during a surprise visit with troops, November 27, 2003. (U.S. National Archives and Presidential Libraries)

Bush stunned hundreds of service members when he appeared at their Thanksgiving dinner, emerging to cheers and applause before helping serve a traditional holiday meal and posing for photos with troops stationed far from home.

"I can't think of a finer group of folks to have Thanksgiving dinner with than you all," Bush told the troops at the time.

"We're proud of you," he added. "Today, Americans are gathering with their loved ones to give thanks for the many blessings in our lives."

News of his trip was not even released until Air Force One had already left Iraq.

US FACES ANOTHER MAJOR MIDDLE EAST TROOP WITHDRAWAL, BUT THREAT REMAINS

President George W. Bush carries a platter of turkey and fixings as he visits US troops for Thanksgiving in Baghdad, 27 November, 2003. Bush made the Thanksgiving visit to troops in Baghdad under the greatest secrecy and security. (ANJA NIEDRINGHAUS/AP POOL/AFP via Getty Images)

Bush's visit came as American forces battled a growing insurgency following the fall of Saddam Hussein's government earlier that year. Despite Bush's declaration in May that major combat operations had ended, U.S. troops continued to face deadly attacks as the military worked to stabilize the country.

Just weeks later, U.S. forces captured Saddam Hussein in December 2003.

#5824. Strobe Talbott, former deputy secretary of state and Brookings Institution president, dies at 80

Strobe Talbott, a former deputy secretary of state who once worked at Time magazine, died at age 80.

Strobe Talbott, a former deputy secretary of state whose deep expertise on the Soviet Union shaped U.S. foreign policy before he went on to lead the Brookings Institution, died Thursday at the age of 80.

Talbott’s son, Devin Talbott, said his father died peacefully after a long illness at his home in East Hampton, New York, The Associated Press reported.

An Ohio native, Talbott began his journalism career as an intern at Time magazine while studying at Yale and Oxford, where he was roommates with future President Bill Clinton. Clinton later joked that his job in their shared apartment was to make Talbott breakfast every morning.

NATO WARNS PUTIN COULD STRIKE BEFORE EUROPEAN ARMY RESPONDS, POINTS TO US AS MILITARY MODEL

Strobe Talbott, president of the Brookings Institution, right, and Wolfgang Ischinger, chairman of the Munich Security Conference on Security Policy, left, participate in a news conference in Berlin on July 16, 2008. (AP Photo/Franka Bruns)

While at Time, Talbott was tapped to translate the leaked memoirs of Nikita Khrushchev, the former Soviet leader ousted in 1964, which earned him a full-time role at the magazine.

Over a 21-year career there, he focused on U.S.-Soviet relations, led the Washington bureau, and wrote as a foreign affairs columnist.

In 1994, Talbott joined the Clinton administration as deputy secretary of state, helping guide U.S. relations with Russia and other post-Soviet states.

In a statement, Bill Clinton said no one was better suited to advise him on Russian affairs than Talbott, noting that former Secretary of State Hillary Clinton also relied on his counsel.

TRUMP PUSHED NATO TO SPEND BIG — NOW COMES THE HARDER QUESTION: CAN EUROPE ACTUALLY FIGHT?

Strobe Talbott, president of the Brookings Institution, speaks during a news conference in Berlin on July 16, 2008. (AP Photo/Franka Bruns)

"He was analytical, completely devoted to diplomacy, and on the level," Bill Clinton said. "With his expertise, we made real progress building trust between two former adversaries — moving beyond decades of confrontation to reduce the nuclear dangers future generations would inherit and build a Europe where more people could live in freedom and peace, including through the expansion of NATO and resolving ethnic conflicts in the Balkans. Together we laid the groundwork for greater cooperation while putting Russia on a path to democracy."

"Sadly, its current leader has taken the country in a different direction," Clinton added, referring to Russian President Vladimir Putin.

FILE - President-elect Bill Clinton and Strobe Talbott of Time magazine talk privately as they walk along the beach on Hilton Head Island, S.C., Jan. 2, 1993. (AP Photo/James Finley)

He described Talbott as a "good and brilliant man who made our world more peaceful and secure," and said, "Hillary and I will miss him very much."

After leaving government, Talbott served as president of the Brookings Institution from 2002 to 2017 and chaired the State Department’s Foreign Affairs Policy Board.

President Bill Clinton during his last State of the Union Address before Congress. (Douglas Graham/Congressional Quarterly/Getty Images)

He is survived by two sons, Devin and Adrian, and six grandchildren. His first wife, Brooke Shearer, died in 2009; he later married author Barbara Lazear Ascher.

The Associated Press contributed to this report.

#5819. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5812. The U.S. government is investigating Iranian ties to as many as 50 hacked commercial vessels

In an aerial view, a container ship arrives at the Port of Oakland on Oct. 10, 2025 in Oakland, Calif. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images hide caption

The U.S. government has intelligence suggesting that up to 50 commercial shipping vessels were recently compromised by cyberattacks, activity federal officials are investigating as potentially linked to Iran.

That's according to a U.S. official familiar with the matter who was not authorized to speak on the record, a former military cyber operator and an industry executive briefed on the attacks. The series of breaches was first reported by the The Wall Street Journal.

The attacks against the maritime sector, if officials' suspicions are correct, may be the latest example of Iranian cyberattacks against global critical infrastructure during the ongoing war with the United States.

NPR has reported that cybersecurity experts and U.S. officials believe Iranian hackers were responsible for a series of digital attacks against U.S. water treatment systems across the country in August as well as a pattern of attacks targeting refrigeration cooling systems that led to spoiled food at grocery stores on U.S. military bases and all across Europe as recently as early November.

The Coast Guard and the FBI first partnered in August to send a "highly specialized team" to board two different foreign commercial vessels "in the Gulf of America" heading to U.S. ports, according to a statement provided by the FBI and the Coast Guard. "The captain, crew, and shore-side corporate staff were critical partners in helping to ensure the threats were mitigated," according to the statement.

The FBI and Coast Guard press offices also told NPR that there are no current "reports of operational disruptions, vessel instability, physical danger to crews, or environmental impacts."

However, there are dozens more ships in international waters that the U.S. government believes have been compromised by hackers and many others who might be vulnerable to similar attacks.

According to the U.S. official familiar with the investigation, bad actors took advantage of known vulnerabilities in Fortinet FortiGate devices, which are frequently used in the maritime industry. The Coast Guard and FBI released a cyber bulletin in September warning the industry that bad actors were breaking into these devices, which help manage firewalls and virtual private networks. "Threat actors routinely scan for specific, unpatched vulnerabilities to gain a foothold," the bulletin read.

According to the U.S. official, in some cases, employees aboard the ships suspected something nefarious was going on when they noticed email disruptions or couldn't log in to their accounts, or, in at least one case, an extended communications blackout. The U.S. official declined to comment on how the U.S. government knows about the extent of breaches across the sector.

However, according to the U.S. official, hackers were able to break in using the Fortinet vulnerability and then pivot from the ships' IT systems over to their network of devices controlling physical processes on the ships, what's called operational technology.

The U.S. official said the bad actors "could have impacted the systems on board that control propulsion, steering, navigation, and cargo management," though they did not share any examples of that kind of activity occurring. In at least one case, one of the ships affected was an LNG tanker designed to transport liquified natural gas. It's important to keep systems at a certain temperature to prevent dangerous accidents if a ship's cargo includes explosive materials –from chemicals to fuel.

While commercial vessels are accustomed to physical security reviews entering the United States following the Sept. 11 terrorist attacks, digital threats are becoming all the more important to track as adversaries can break in and cause damage from afar. "It's a totally new paradigm that these tankers are networked in this way," the U.S. official said.

Even so, national security officials have feared something like this might happen for years. A former Navy official who requested anonymity to discuss internal operations tells NPR that "we had been tracking the possibility to exploit and take control of [operational technology] systems on Navy ships and Merchant [Marine] ships for a long time, and now this is coming to fruition."

While the U.S. government is wary of advertising Iranian hacking efforts during the war, officials also want to spread awareness of the vulnerability of internet connected devices, particularly those that are linked to the technology that controls critical processes. "These are not sophisticated attacks," the official said. "This is a known vulnerability that could be mitigated with some pretty basic cyber hygiene."

"Modern ships are highly integrated systems. A cyberattack presents significant risks, including the potential for a collision, grounding, oil spill, or even an explosion. The Coast Guard regularly publishes bulletins on cyber vulnerabilities and actions vessel owners and operators can take to secure their networks," according to a statement from the Coast Guard.

Ahead of the winter and a busy holiday season for commercial shipping, officials are bracing themselves to investigate any ship entering U.S. ports that could be compromised.

"People never think about what a modern blessing electricity is until it goes out. People take the marine transportation system for granted until there's a problem and their stuff doesn't get there by Christmas," the U.S. official said. "I wish more Americans knew how complex this system is."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5805. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick, R-Pa., speaks at a House Foreign Affairs subcommittee hearing in Washington on Sept. 21, 2022. Samuel Corum/Getty Images hide caption

#5777. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5774. Group of Senate Democrats push to add freshman to leadership

U.S. Senate Majority Leader Charles Schumer (D-NY), center, is seen here meeting with newly elected Democratic Senators, (L-R) Senator-elect Andy Kim (D-NJ), Senator-elect Ruben Gallego (D-AZ), Senator-elect Angela Alsobrooks (D-MD) Senator-elect Adam Schiff (D-CA) Senator-elect Lisa Blunt Rochester (D-DE) and Senator-elect Elissa Slotkin (D-MI) at the U.S. Capitol Building on November 12, 2024. That group of senators is pushing to add an incoming freshman senator to the leadership ranks. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images/Getty Images North America hide caption

"Leader Schumer was already planning to do this and is incredibly supportive of the idea," a spokesperson told NPR.

On Tuesday, Van Hollen posted to social media: "There should be a freshman senator on the Senate leadership team. As I've said, we need a new way to organize so we can deliver for the American people."
