#5771. U.S. Judge rules FCC violated ABC's free speech rights to please Trump

Under Chairman Brendan Carr, left, the Federal Communications Commission launched an early review of Disney's licenses to operate all the local ABC stations it owns. New Disney CEO Josh D'Amaro, right, says the company is fighting back to protect ABC's journalistic integrity and free speech. "We're very principled on this," he said. Heather Diehl/Thibault Camus/Pool/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

A federal judge has blocked President Trump and his administration from taking action to challenge the ABC television network's broadcast licenses, finding it to be an unconstitutional punishment for satire, commentary and news coverage that Trump doesn't like.

In her preliminary injunction filed Wednesday, U.S. District Court Judge Loren AliKhan found that the federal agency's early reviews represented retaliation for speech the Trump administration resented.

Such perceived offenses included late-night host Jimmy Kimmel's jabs at the president, the critical remarks of hosts on the chat show The View, and the moderation of a presidential debate in September 2024.

ABC and its corporate parent, the Walt Disney Co., serve six of the 10 largest markets in the U.S., including New York, Los Angeles, and Houston. They contribute significantly to the network's bottom line.

"Given the extreme, indeed existential, nature of the possible penalty Plaintiffs face -- loss of their broadcast licenses -- the court concludes that early license renewal proceedings create a risk sufficient to chill a broadcaster of ordinary firmness," Judge AliKhan wrote.

She rejected the Trump administration's motion to dismiss the lawsuit from ABC and Disney challenging the review of the local licenses. Usually the licenses last eight years and are up for review on a staggered basis. They are rarely taken away.

In her ruling, AliKhan relied heavily on public statements and social media postings in which Trump attacked ABC and Disney, as well as posts by Trump's pick as the chair of the Federal Communications Commission, Brendan Carr.

"What started in 2020 as questions about how ABC 'can keep getting a license' if it 'report[s] fake news,' ... has crescendoed into repeated calls for the Commission to strip ABC's broadcast licenses for their editorial decisions," she wrote. "The court counts eleven such calls made by President Trump over the past two years -- the most recent being in July 2026."

And the judge cited the administration's "tangible steps toward enforcing President Trump and Commissioner Carr's repeated threats to strip the Plaintiff Stations of their licenses."

"Chairman Carr has taken President Trump's rhetoric as his marching orders," Judge AliKhan wrote.

Carr did not respond to NPR's request for comment Wednesday evening.

ABC hailed the ruling in a statement as "an important victory for the First Amendment."

Among the targets of ire from Trump and Carr was ABC's decision not to broadcast Trump's July 2026 Oval Office address on its television network. ABC's legal team said the network streamed it on its digital news service, but would not have done so absent the political pressure from the FCC.

It is one of several examples of newsroom decisions made under duress, the network's lawyers said in court. Kimmel's interview with then-Democratic U.S. Senate nominee James Talarico of Texas was uploaded to YouTube instead of being aired on ABC's late night television show. (Talarico won election to the U.S. Senate earlier this month.) And The View stopped hosting candidates for political office in 2026 in the months ahead of the fall elections.

Judge AliKhan rejected arguments from the FCC that the court could not take action because it has not yet rejected the license renewals. The very act of premature review for retaliatory reasons is unconstitutional and unlawful – and hard to reverse, she wrote.

"For nearly two years, the agency has weaponized its licensing authority to retaliate against broadcasters for exercising their right to free expression, and now a federal court has stepped in to put a check on that conduct," said Anna M. Gomez, the lone Democratic commissioner on the FCC. "Every broadcaster facing this pressure should take note and choose courage over capitulation because, as I've said from the beginning, the First Amendment is on their side."

This ruling comes six weeks after a parallel ruling in a case brought by CNN, MS NOW and Politico against the Trump administration for restricting access. Officials barred journalists from the three news outlets from the White House after Trump lambasted them for "purposefully negative" news coverage.

In that case, U.S. District Court Judge Timothy Kelly issued a preliminary injunction, writing, "It is not hard to conclude that President Trump likely banned Plaintiffs from the White House because he believes that their reporting is generally inaccurate and biased against him." That, he wrote, represents a form of unlawful retaliation. The administration is appealing. And despite the ruling, the White House continues to limit the three news outlets' access to the president.

In December 2024, just a few weeks before Trump became president for the second time, Disney agreed to settle a lawsuit Trump had brought against ABC News by paying $16 million, including legal fees. Many outside legal experts believed Disney would have had the upper hand if it had stuck it out in court.

Now under new leadership, Disney has drawn the line at what it contends is a threat to its broadcast licenses — and profits — over its editorial independence.

"We're very principled on this," Disney CEO Josh D'Amaro told CNBC in August, just days before the company filed its lawsuit. "We're going to stand up to what we believe is journalistic integrity, and we're not going to be told how to run that side of our business." D'Amaro became chief executive in March.

The FCC had already kicked off an investigation of Disney's diversity practices when it announced the review.

Carr, the agency's chief, has said it initiated the review of Disney's broadcast licenses over concerns that the company engaged in racial discrimination through its diversity, equity and inclusion actions.

"Obviously, the Disney lawsuit is without merit," Carr told NPR in August. "From our perspective, we're going to continue to follow the facts and the law wherever they may lead."

Judge AliKhan wrote in her order that Disney had been responsive to every query that the FCC had made in the diversity investigation. Carr said in a statement to a congresswoman that he found the responses to be "deficient, nonresponsive, and disingenuous."

The FCC generally reviews broadcast licenses once every eight years. The decision to examine Disney's licenses now is remarkable in two ways: Reviews of a company's licenses are usually staggered, rather than all at once. Additionally, some of Disney's licenses are not due for renewal for another several years. It is considered an essentially unprecedented act for the FCC to take.

In a court hearing in October, Disney's legal team argued it was suffering irreparable harm to its First Amendment freedoms as a result of the swift move to review the licenses.

Lead Disney attorney Beth Wilkinson compared the reviews to a "guillotine blade" hanging above the company, ready to fall at any moment. (Judge AliKhan thanked her for not comparing it to the Sword of Damocles of Greek legend.)

U.S. Justice Department attorney Dimitar Georgiev-Remmel shrugged off that claim. But his arguments led to a series of telling and dramatic exchanges with the judge.

Georgiev-Remmel contended that the review was a useful "tool" the FCC was deploying to secure more complete information for the diversity investigation. He said the agency was having trouble eliciting necessary information from Disney.

Disney's Wilkinson seized on the claim, asserting it proved that the license review was unnecessary. And she noted that the FCC only informed Disney that it was dissatisfied with the information the company had provided about its diversity practices 17 days after the reviews had been announced. That gave the company little time to stave off the nearly unprecedented process by fulfilling the requests.

Judge AliKhan asked Georgiev-Remmel what other tools the agency had at its disposal to get that information.

The Justice Department trial lawyers representing the FCC appeared to be caught off guard. Georgiev-Remmel initially said he wasn't sure.

Some minutes later, after conferring with colleagues, Georgiev-Remmel said the FCC could have subpoenaed the information, fined Disney, or taken other measures. But he said the agency could decide to set the reviews aside should Disney provide sufficient information.

The judge appeared immediately skeptical that the instant license reviews were a necessary or even reasonable step to achieve that.

In both Disney's lawsuit against the FCC and CNN, MS NOW and Politico's suit against the White House, the media companies argued that the Trump administration had violated their constitutional rights of free speech and due process. The Justice Department argued other priorities and prerogatives were in play: national security in one, the need to pursue an investigation into diversity practices in the other.

And in both, the judges expressed incredulity or skepticism that the Trump administration had acted in good faith.

As in matters beyond the realm of media, the Trump administration is pressing to expand its authority and powers. The White House access case would effectively require a reversal of decades of precedent on closely related cases.

In the case of ABC, the Trump administration wants the ability to trip up broadcasters who put things on the air that offend the president – simply because that technology falls under government regulation.

Even during all this litigation, the Justice Department initiated an anti-trust investigation into the five networks – including ABC and CNN – that make up the White House video pool sharing footage and duties to other outlets. The networks have been collaborating since their inception.

What caught the attention of the administration? The fact that the networks consulted before deciding not to substitute another channel in for CNN -- on the increasing number of days the president's aides blocked it from serving as the pool representative covering Trump's public appearances in small spaces that cannot accommodate the large contingent of reporters that cover the White House.

#5762. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5755. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5746. Nextnova unveils solar cell manufacturing facility

The new state-of-the-art factory in Oklahoma City will create about 300 jobs, strengthen the domestic solar supply chain, and establish a new benchmark for transparency and quality.

This month, Nextnova celebrated the official inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City, marking an important milestone in strengthening America’s domestic solar manufacturing industry.

The facility will manufacture high-efficiency solar cells using advanced automation, Industry 4.0 technologies, and comprehensive digital traceability.

Production is scheduled to begin in March 2027 with an initial annual capacity of 2 GW, designed to expand to 5 GW as market demand grows.

The new factory is expected to create approximately 300 skilled jobs in engineering, manufacturing, quality assurance, and operations, supporting economic growth while reinforcing the U.S. clean energy supply chain.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

“Today represents an exciting milestone for Nextnova and for American solar manufacturing,” said Nathan Kii Miller, Nextnova’s chief strategy officer, in a statement. “Our Oklahoma City facility combines advanced manufacturing technology, complete supply chain transparency, and rigorous quality standards to deliver premium solar cells while strengthening a resilient domestic solar industry.”

Designed for maximum quality and reliability, the facility features fully integrated manufacturing execution systems (MES), automated inspection technologies, advanced process control, and a dedicated analytical laboratory supporting continuous quality verification throughout production.

Nextnova notes that its commitment to transparent sourcing, manufacturing excellence, and scalable production provides customers with high-performance solar cells that support the continued growth of a secure and competitive U.S. solar industry.

#5737. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5736. New Jersey utilities have less than a year to implement virtual power plants

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Data from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ will inform the development of a long-term, open-access virtual power plant tariff, state utility regulators said on Nov. 12.

PowerUp NJ is a “first-of-its-kind statewide virtual power plant program that will give ratepayers more control to drive down their own energy costs,” Sherrill said in a statement that also referred to the plan as a “voluntary community energy program” that allows customers to “lend power from their homes” to support the grid.

The BPU’s order this month cements the program features and goals outlined in a July straw proposal from the agency.

Among other things, the order envisions a market-based framework that compensates distributed energy resources for grid services, rather than subsidizing their deployment; has standard program design elements across all four regulated utility territories; is technology neutral, with eligibility extended to net-metered solar-plus-storage systems; and requires utilities to test localized dispatch with an eye towards developing grid services that could defer or avoid infrastructure upgrades, according to a program factsheet.

The order also establishes technical working groups on measurement and verification, cost-benefit analysis, and program marketing; and aims to coordinate existing utility and state programs and proceedings involving distribution system planning, net metering, energy efficiency and electric vehicles, according to the factsheet.

The BPU expects smart thermostats, stationary home batteries, internet-connected electric water heaters and vehicle chargers and electric vehicles themselves to be eligible for PowerUp NJ at launch. Participating utilities and manufacturers will notify customers of specific eligible devices and models in mid-2027.

PowerUp NJ will be an opt-in program, meaning customers must affirmatively choose to participate. Once enrolled, customers will be able to “choose which devices and events to include” to avoid inconvenient or uncomfortable situations, like ceding control of a thermostat on a hot day, the BPU said.

Like other state-level VPPs, such as California’s Demand Side Grid Support program, PowerUp NJ aims to reduce grid stress and mitigate power price spikes by shifting load away from demand peaks. It will target both “distribution-level grid stress events” and PJM capacity and transmission coincident peaks, according to the program factsheet.

A preliminary staff analysis found that a 3% cut to peak demand could avoid about $60 million in annual system capacity costs while cutting local and greenhouse gas emissions from fossil-fueled peaker plants in New Jersey, the BPU said.

In a statement, BPU President Ben Hertz-Shargel said his agency would apply lessons from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ to the development of a long-term framework for the program.

“We will develop this program carefully, learn from real-world results, and expand it over time, growing the earnings opportunities for residential and business customers,” Hertz-Shargel said in a statement.

#5731. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

#5724. DOJ closes NextEra-Dominion antitrust probe as state hearings continue

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The decision followed a promise by the utilities to allow ISO-NE’s internal market monitor to investigate proposed plant retirements.

In a July filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Dominion said the merger needs approval from FERC, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the SCC, the North Carolina Utilities Commission and the Public Service Commission of South Carolina.

The SCC has been holding daily evidentiary hearings since Nov. 17, while South Carolina and North Carolina regulators have scheduled theirs to begin in December and February, respectively. Neither FERC nor the NRC have issued a decision.

Dominion and NextEra announced in a Sept. 14 release that they would double a proposed monthly residential bill credit for Virginia customers from two to four years.

In that release, the companies also said they would accelerate solar, storage and other resources in Virginia to “affordably power the Commonwealth’s growth,” advance the Virginia Clean Economy Act and deliver “long-term affordability benefits by leveraging the combined company’s scale to buy, build, finance and operate more efficiently.”

Last week, SCC regulators and attorneys for the state pressed witnesses, including Baine, NextEra CEO John Ketchum and Dominion Energy CEO Bob Blue, on how customer benefits could be calculated and ensured.

Baine said that when Dominion merged with SCANA in 2019, it saw “benefits of scale” where input cost was lowered, adding, “We believe it will be on a much bigger scale with this merger, but just can't do the math for you today.”

John Farmer, a senior assistant attorney general for Virginia, pointed Ketchum to a written response from Dominion and NextEra in which they said they “have not prepared a year-by-year quantification of benefits to Dominion Energy Virginia's existing customers.”

Ketchum said he stands behind “the fact that we've offered up the bill credits” and broadly outlined the potential operational benefits of a combined company.

“Shame on us if we can't realize the scale benefits that I've spoken about,” Ketchum said in Wednesday’s hearing. “That’s what we do.”

SCC Chair Kelsey Bagot asked Blue how he would ensure that “potential issues do not erode the benefits” that are “possible” as a result of the merger.

“Have you started developing a plan?” Bagot said.

Blue said that if the merger isn’t managed well, “it is possible that the longer-term benefits that we're describing won't materialize as quickly.”

“As I mentioned in my answer to you before, I don't believe that would leave our customers worse off,” he continued. “There may be some missed opportunities.”

Some intervenors also criticized the bill credit proposal.

Bloom Advisory Partners partner Teresa Ringenbach testified on behalf of the Retail Energy Advancement League that the bill credit structure “effectively penalizes customers for choosing a competitive supplier,” which she said “is discriminatory and undermines the statutorily mandated retail choice framework.”

“A large general service customer using 72,000 megawatt hours per year would receive approximately $225,000 if it shops, compared to approximately $642,000 if it does not,” Ringenbach said. “That difference is a price signal, and it’s funded by the utility's new parent, and it rewards remaining on the utility's generation service.”

“Shareholder credits should not be viewed as the price of getting a merger approved,” she added. “They should deliver real value to every single wires customer.”

Bagot asked Richard McCann, an energy expert testifying on behalf of Gov. Abigail Spanberger’s office, “Do we have the ability to provide rate credits to a single rate class? For example … only to residential customers and not to any of the other rate classes.”

“I recognize that's not exactly what you're proposing,” Bagot added, “but it does shift potentially what that rate credit would be, maybe closer to $29 per month.”

Raising the credit to $29 a month was a condition McCann proposed in his written testimony, along with a six-year residential rate freeze.

In response to Bagot, McCann said, “I know that at $10, they won't notice if it phases out or not. $30 a month, they might notice that the credit disappears if it's a sudden shock.”

He said that implementing the higher credit for residential ratepayers alongside other methods he proposed — such as raising the debt share of Dominion's capital structure — could lower bills over time and provide a “smooth glide path” for the end of the credits.

SCC Commissioner Jehmal Hudson pushed back on the rate freeze proposal, asking McCann what authority allows the SCC to “freeze future base rate increases irrespective of the future costs” and noting that rate freezes in Virginia “have primarily stemmed from the General Assembly legislation.”

“You are in an extraordinary position right now with this merger,” McCann said. “There are certain things that you have discretion about going forward — you will have to decide if you have that discretion.”

#5707. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5703. Off-grid costs will push data centers back to utilities: Mitsubishi Heavy Industries CEO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CEO Eisaku Ito expressed confidence in utilities’ long-term expansion plans, arguing that growing demand for electricity does not rely solely on data centers.

Despite the recent attention on new electric demand created by data centers, Mitsubishi Heavy Industries sees greater potential for long-term growth in the utilities sector, MHI President and CEO Eisaku Ito said earlier this month.

Utilities and independent power producers accounted for 94% of Mitsubishi Heavy Industries’ new gas turbine orders in 2024 and 2025, Ito said.

Although many U.S. data center operators say they intend to install their own off-grid generation, Ito argued that these plans rely on smaller gas-fired power plants that cost more to run than the larger plants utilities and IPPs are pursuing.

In the long-term, Ito said, the cost differential will push data centers to connect to the grid, ultimately supporting utilities' expansion plans.

His comments, made during a call with analysts on Nov. 6, according to a translated transcript, come as fallout from the U.S. midterm elections and stock market volatility raise questions about the future of the artificial intelligence buildout in North America. Ito expressed confidence in utilities' long-term expansion plans, arguing that growing demand for electricity does not rely solely on data centers.

“Electricity demand from data centers is expected to grow rapidly in the United States, but data centers still account for only around 10% of the country's total electricity demand,” Ito said. “If some of the anticipated growth in demand does not materialize, there will be some impact, but we believe the effect on our overall GTCC business would be limited.”

MHI booked 8 GW in orders for large frame gas turbines in the first half of its 2026 fiscal year, compared to 16 GW of orders in all of FY 2025. Profit for the company’s Energy Systems segment rose 80% on account of increased orders and shifts in the value of the yen, according to the company's financial statements.

“We are also beginning to see the benefits of improved pricing on new unit projects, lower loss-related costs and greater production efficiency,” MHI CFO Hiroshi Nishio said.

Company executives said they continue to take a conservative approach to forecasting demand and expanding manufacturing capacity. The company expects global demand for gas turbines at all manufacturers and makers to average about 70 GW per year over the next five years — somewhat lower than the 100 GW MHI expects will sell across all manufacturers this year amid what Ito characterized as “exceptionally strong” demand he does not anticipate will continue.

But Ito said he remains confident in MHI’s company backlog, noting that the company vets new orders carefully and hasn’t had any order cancellations in the past two years.

The company plans to double its gas turbine production capacity by 2030, though most of the additional capacity will come from improved processes and automation at existing plants in the U.S. and Japan.

“We are improving productivity and driving other initiatives at a pace that outstrips the growth in orders,” he said.

MHI also signaled that it's exploring opportunities to expand into other infrastructure-related markets.

Ito said MHI will release additional details in May, but suggested the company is interested in expanding its gas turbine service and repair capabilities, as well as investing in unmanned systems that can operate in harsh environments. At the same time, MHI plans to divest some of its aerospace business, he said.

“These examples may seem quite different, but they all share a common theme: resilience,” Ito said. “We aim to identify new business opportunities in infrastructure and industrial machinery through the lens of resilience and turn them into future growth.”

#5683. Midterms provide clarity on data center development: Vistra CEO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“As we’ve said for some time, load interconnect queues were overstated across the country and especially in ERCOT, driven by speculation from developers,” Vistra CEO James Burke said.

The midterm elections — with data centers a key issue in many races — helped provide clarity around the environment for data center development, James Burke, Vistra president and CEO, said on an earnings call earlier this month.

“Before the elections, in many markets, we were seeing concerns about data centers in the way that the public was reacting to the pace of development, who had control over those decisions, what impact could it have on electric prices, water, all of that,” Burke said on the Nov. 6 call. “Moving past this election is a positive just in terms of being able to understand what's at stake, and now how do we move forward?”

So far, the key messages from Texas Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa have been “very similar to some of the key messages that Governor Abbott had expressed and … consistent with messages we've been expressing, which is there has to be some guardrails on this development,” Burke said.

Vistra wants tougher requirements for data center interconnection, including higher collateral requirements and take-or-pay agreements for transmission charges, according to Burke.

“As we've said for some time, load interconnect queues were overstated across the country and especially in [the Electric Reliability Council of Texas footprint], driven by speculation from developers,” Burke said.

Even with the midterm elections and regulatory actions around data centers, through 2030 Vistra expects load to grow by 4% to 6% a year in ERCOT and by 2% to 3% annually in the PJM Interconnection footprint, according to Burke.

Despite Vistra’s expectations for load growth in ERCOT, power prices there have been trending lower due to some crypto mining facilities coming offline so they can convert into data centers, and the state’s data center audit and permitting pause, Burke said.

“We do not see this as a long-term impact,” he said, noting that ERCOT hit a 91 GW peak demand record this year.

Vistra’s fleet has grown to about 50 GW with its Oct. 30 Cogentrix acquisition, Burke said.

The fleet includes combined cycle and peaking power plants that are running more often, according to Burke.

“As renewable penetration continues throughout the country, you're seeing these assets get called on more,” Burke said in response to an analyst question about potentially selling off parts of the fleet. “So we've been bullish on dispatchable. We've been bullish on flexibility … So as a long-term owner-operator, we like the fleet.”

#5682. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5680. FERC must take action to protect ratepayers from higher transmission costs in SPP

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Southwest Power Pool is misusing an exemption meant for urgent reliability needs to avoid competition in favor of incumbent utilities, writes Paul Cicio.

Paul Cicio is chairman of the Electricity Transmission Competition Coalition and president and CEO of the Industrial Energy Consumers of America.

As electricity prices continue to surge, consumers cannot afford to pay more than necessary for the new transmission lines that deliver their power. Requiring utilities to compete with one another gives regulators a direct way to reduce those costs.

Earlier this year, the Southwest Power Pool decided to exempt four projects expected to cost $3.8 billion from competitive bidding by classifying them as short-term reliability projects under its 2025 Integrated Transmission Planning portfolio.

SPP’s tariff specifies that unless a project is required to be in service in three years or less, it must face competition in building new large transmission lines, consistent with Federal Energy Regulatory Commission Order 1000. But the projects SPP has designated as short-term in its 2025 portfolio are not expected to enter service until 2029 or 2030.

SPP violated its FERC-approved tariff by not requiring the transmission projects to face competition. It is misusing an exemption meant for urgent reliability needs to avoid competition in favor of incumbent utilities, contrary to the vital electricity affordability needs of ratepayers. As a result, ratepayers in the 17-state region could pay an estimated $750 million in higher electricity bills for these projects.

We at the Electricity Transmission Competition Coalition and the Industrial Energy Consumers of America have filed a complaint asking FERC to hold SPP accountable for violating its tariff and require the four projects to be competitively bid.

Ratepayers are concerned because SPP has relied on this exemption before and the projects have grown larger and more expensive over time.

In 2024, SPP directly assigned $3.2 billion in projects to incumbent utilities based on short-term reliability need, without competitive bidding. The Industrial Energy Consumers of America opposed those projects in a January 2025 letter to SPP.

This past January, the ETCC urged the SPP Board of Directors to put its 2025 short-term reliability projects, which included new 765-kV lines, through competitive bidding. But SPP has continued to bypass competition over these and other stakeholder objections without providing sufficient justification or transparency.

If FERC allows SPP to continue, the short-term reliability exemption could become a routine way to avoid competition altogether, and the precedent would reach ratepayers across the country.

When utilities are required to compete with one another, they sharpen their pencils and contractually commit to cost containment provisions. Some include provisions that guarantee delivery of the project on time or pay a financial penalty.

None of the $3.2 billion projects SPP directly assigned in 2024 included a guaranteed in-service deadline or cost containment provisions.

Competition works. ETCC reviewed 19 transmission projects that went through competitive bidding and found an average cost reduction of 34%. ETCC’s review of 15 projects that did not go through competitive bidding found an average cost overrun of 89%.

Also, there is no evidence that assigning projects directly to incumbent utilities gets them built faster. For example, SPP competitively bid the Minco and Wolf Creek projects, which entered into service in 2025 and met their needed or expected in-service dates.

Competition can deliver on both reduced costs and on time start-up of the project. Ratepayers do not have to sacrifice reliability for lower costs.

The nearly $3.8 billion price tag is only the beginning of what consumers could ultimately pay. When financing costs, utility profits and other expenses are factored in, the total cost to ratepayers could more than double over the life of these projects.

SPP's decisions today will shape electricity costs for families and businesses across the region for decades to come. And SPP is poised to develop an even costlier transmission plan for 2027 that includes billions of dollars more in projects that would be exempted from competition.

The Federal Power Act is first and foremost a consumer protection act and FERC has a responsibility to ensure that consumers receive just and reasonable rates. FERC approved SPP’s tariff and has a duty to enforce it.

Electricity consumers will ultimately pay for the billions of dollars that grid operators commit to new transmission. FERC should hold SPP to competitive bidding as the standard for that spending, with exemptions limited to the narrow cases for projects that can be completed and placed into service in three years or less.

The SPP complaint is yet another test for FERC. Will the commission stand with consumers or protect monopoly utility profits? There is a troubling pattern of inaction by FERC to enforce Order 1000. Consumers have filed four complaints at FERC dating back to 2022 without them taking action, despite electricity affordability being top of mind nationwide.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

PJM had planned to accept offers for backstop power supplies from Sept. 30 through Oct. 21 after falling short of its reserve margin targets. But FERC only partially approved its plan, zeroing in on cost allocation concerns.

#5678. Three Green Hydrogen Projects Slip Through The Trump Chopper

When US President Donald Trump swept into office last year, he set his choppers upon the $8 billion Regional Clean Hydrogen Hubs program, snuffing out plans to establish a diversified, domestic hydrogen market with a focus on decarbonization. Nevertheless, some stakeholders have continued to pursue the dream of producing green hydrogen from renewable resources — instead of squeezing it from natural gas. Now all that hard work is beginning to pay off, Trump or no Trump.

If you guessed the startup Plug Power is in the mix, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. Plug Power began life back in 1997 with a focus on electric forklifts powered by zero emission hydrogen fuel cells. In addition to replacing fossil fuels, Plug Power aimed to replace battery-powered forklifts, with fuel cells being a better fit for the fast-paced environment of shipping and logistics.

Plug Power soon pivoted into replacing gas-derived hydrogen fuel with so-named green hydrogen, pushed from water by an electrical current. After a series of ups and downs the company hit the jackpot in January of 2025, when the US Department of Energy tapped it for a $1.66 loan guarantee in support of the Regional Clean Hydrogen Hubs program, which was funded through the 2021 Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. If all went according to plan, Plug Power would have used the loan to finance up to six green hydrogen projects around the US, all affiliated with the Hydrogen Hubs program.

So much for that. The Energy Department announced the loan award at the beginning of the month, when former President Joe Biden was still in office. However, by the end of the month Trump was officially installed in the White House. He soon pulled the plug on the Plug Power loan, so to speak, sending it down the drain alongside many other federally funded clean power projects.

That was not the end of the story for Plug Power. After all, it’s a big world out there, and Plug Power continued to pitch its wares overseas. Last week the company also relaunched itself back into the US market, bigly.

On November 17, Plug Power announced that its new “GenEco™ Scale” green hydrogen electrolyzer system is up for grabs in the US market while concurrently announcing that its first customer is the Texas-based firm Aracdia eFuels. Arcadia will deploy 280 megawatts’ worth of GenEco Scale modules at its Project ARC campus in the Portland/Corpus Christi area in Texas. When fully up and running, Project ARC will deploy green hydrogen from the GenEco Scale system to produce about 23.2 million gallons of fossil-free aviation fuel per year.

While green hydrogen has its fans in the decarbonization field, the stubbornly high cost of electrolysis has raised a formidable obstacle against widespread commercial uptake. Plug Power is among the stakeholders pitching modular, integrated systems to help bring costs down into parity with conventional hydrogen sourced from natural gas or coal, and that’s where the GenEco system comes into play.

“Built around a standardized 20 MW block architecture, GenEco Scale combines Plug’s proven proton exchange membrane (PEM) electrolyzer technology with integrated balance-of-plant systems into a modular, pre-engineered solution for hydrogen production projects of approximately 100 MW and above,” Plug Power explained in a press statement last week. The company also noted that the “outdoor-ready” system avoids much of the cost and time related to the buildings and other infrastructure required of traditional large scale electrolyzer systems.

Arcadia eFuels CEO Amy Hebert also chipped in her two cents in support of the modular system. “Reducing the engineering burden on our EPC [engineering, procurement, and construction] partners and minimizing the complexity of bringing these systems together were important considerations for Project ARC,” Herbert emphasized.

Savings on land is another part of the cost-cutting plan. Unlike conventional electrolyzer system layouts, the GenEco model deploys a vertical configuration. The vertical system requires less than one acre per 100 megawatts of electrolyzer capacity, according to Plug Power.

Another Plug Power project has bubbled up in California, which was on track to host a suite of projects under the Hydrogen Hubs program with a focus on hydrogen derived from biomass as well as water electrolysis. The funding evaporated under Trump, but the state is still getting its hands on green hydrogen.

On November 19, Plug Power announced that its containerized 5-megawatt GenEco electrolyzer is up and running at a green hydrogen plant in Vernon, California, under the umbrella of the New Jersey-based firm Avina Clean Hydrogen.

Green hydrogen from this project will go to fulfill California’s plans for a zero-emission freight corridor, the plan for which includes fuel cell electric trucks as well as battery-powered trucks. “Located approximately 10 miles from the Port of Long Beach, the facility is designed to serve fleets moving freight through the Los Angeles–Long Beach port complex. It combines on-site fueling with tube-trailer deliveries to customers across the Los Angeles region,” Plug Power explains.

The station, also accommodate buses, and it can refuel a heavy-duty vehicle to more than 95% capacity in less than 30 minutes, according to Plug Power. That stacks up well against charging times for battery-powered heavy duty vehicles. On its website, for example, Tesla asserts that its new Semi electric truck can recover 60% of its 500 mile range in up to 30 minutes at a Tesla Megacharger station.

For the record, Avina has not placed all of its cards on hydrogen fuel cell trucks. The company’s roster of clean hydrogen projects also includes SAF (sustainable aviation fuel) and green ammonia, which is a key input for fertilizer among other chemical products.

Rounding out the trio of new projects is the Swedish firm PowerCell Group. The firm is better known for its work in the marine energy field, and now it is tapping into land-based markets as well. Last week PowerCell announced that its US branch is in motion to set up a green hydrogen system run by excess electricity from an existing solar array owned by a to-be-named client in the US. In effect, the hydrogen will serve as an energy storage medium. It will go to run a PowerCell PowerPod 3000 stationary fuel cell, which will kick into action at night to keep the clean kilowatts flowing after the sun goes down.

PowerCell describes the PowerPod 3000 as “a containerized power generation platform integrating our PS190 systems with power conversion equipment, cooling and DMC, PowerCell’s control and Energy Orchestration technology,” with an installed fuel cell capacity of 27 megawatts and 20 megawatts of rated power.

That’s a good question. Rather than let that excess solar electricity go to waste, the client could invest in a BESS (battery energy storage system) for daytime energy storage and night-time electricity. However, a typical BESS only lasts for a handful of hours. In contrast, PowerCell describes its fuel cell as a “long duration” storage system, which generally means eight hours or more.

PowerCell also points out that a fuel cell does something that a BESS cannot. Fuel cells generate electricity by combining hydrogen with oxygen in the presence of a catalyst. Water — H2O — is a byproduct. All else being equal, that opens up the potential for green hydrogen to serve as a water distribution medium, sucking up water in an electrolysis system at one location, then pushing it out in a fuel cell located elsewhere.

PowerCell is not making any specific promises for this particular project, though the company does emphasize that “the ability to generate both electricity and recoverable water adds another dimension of value to hydrogen-based energy storage.”

“The amount of water recovered, and its potential uses will depend on the final system configuration, operating conditions and water treatment requirements,” the company adds.

And, that’s where things get interesting. The client is a to-be-named electric cooperative, which is of interest because electric cooperatives (more specifically, Rural Electric Cooperatives) account for a large portion of the US grid, and their roots in the rural poverty fighting programs of the Great Depression provide for a distinctive public benefit mission that can, in some cases, allow more wiggle room for innovative energy solutions that generate broad benefits for local communities.

I’m reaching out to PowerCell for more details. If they can’t divulge the name of the client yet, perhaps they’ll be able to describe what they mean by long duration energy storage, so stay tuned for an update.

Photo: The startup Plug Power is among the green hydrogen stakeholders carving out more space for renewable energy in the US market, despite last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy (cropped, courtesy of Plug Power).

#5677. TVA touts $1.7B data center deal under new large-load tariff

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Separately, the utility recently received the first small modular reactor construction permit from the NRC for a BWRX-300 reactor at the Clinch River Site in Oak Ridge, Tennessee.

The Tennessee Valley Authority is set to receive $1.7 billion in aggregate payments from an unnamed data center customer under its new tariff for data centers above 5 MW, the federal utility said in a Nov. 12 U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filing.

“Capacity commitment charges under the agreement are based on a rate of approximately $1.5 million per MW of requested capacity above 5 MW,” the filing said. “This rate reflects TVA's current cohort-based pricing methodology, which may be revised for future customer cohorts.”

The new tariff took effect Oct. 1 and will be phased in through 2029, the filing said. That phase-in period will apply to existing customers to allow them to retain their existing rate structure for a period of time, the Nashville Scene reported in August.

TVA CEO Mike Skaggs said during a Nov. 13 earnings call that the change to data center rates has supported the utility’s investments.

Those investments include extending the lives of the utility’s existing nuclear units and “helping expand the nuclear fuel and supply hub in the East Tennessee area,” he said.

The utility recently received the first small modular reactor construction permit from the U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission for a BWRX-300 reactor at the Clinch River Site in Oak Ridge, “although TVA has not yet decided whether to construct a reactor at the site,” its filing said.

Skaggs came out of retirement to serve as interim CEO following the retirement of former CEO Don Moul and was instated as TVA’s permanent CEO following a Nov. 10 board meeting, TVA’s vice president of financial planning and investor relations Clifton Lowry said on the call.

TVA’s filing also noted that in August, the utility’s board approved 1.5 GW of new natural gas generation, “targeted to be online in 2032.”

As of Sept. 30, TVA said it “had spent $463 million on long-lead-time equipment associated with previously approved projects, which will now be utilized in connection with the project. TVA could spend up to an additional $3.1 billion on this project.”

That previously approved project is TVA’s defunct Middle Tennessee Energy Solution project.

In August, TVA said its approval of the new 1.5 GW gas facility “closes out” the previous project, “and all funding has been transferred to the New Gas Facility 2032.”

TVA’s President and CFO Tom Rice said that the utility is in a “strong financial position” to executive its $13 billion capital investment plan over the next three years. That investment comes on top of the more than $23 billion in investments since 2020, Rice said.

However, TVA’s filing notes that it “continues to experience supply chain pressures resulting from inflation, tariffs and other trade restrictions, material constraints, and labor availability.”

“These factors have contributed to project delays, limited availability of critical materials, and increased costs for both materials and labor,” the utility said. “TVA anticipates that inflationary and tariff pressures will persist into 2027. Although these challenges have been managed with limited disruption to business operations thus far, continued or escalating pressures could result in more substantial operational impacts and increased pressure on power rates.”

#5676. Pondering The Potential Of Plug-In VPP

I recently discussed the potential of the plug-in VPP from Jackery and Every Electric to transform urban energy storage. But the current program could be just the start. The more I discussed it at RE+, the more it started making me thing about where it could go in the future.

Essentially, customers of the plug-in VPP receive a battery, plug it into the wall and their window air conditioner, and connect to Wi-Fi. Then they use AC as they normally would, the VPP shaves off demand peaks on the grid, and the customer gets paid. It is likely the fastest, easiest and cheapest way to implement urban BESS in the US. And regular people living in apartments benefit. You can read more about the plug-in VPP in my previous article for context. Or you can watch the video below.

Both Jackery and Every Electric are collaborating and working to create innovative solutions for customer needs. Plug-in VPP makes the benefits of battery storage accessible to people in apartments. However, the potential could go much farther. Of note, much of what I am discussing here is not available yet. But the plug-in VPP sparked my imagination, and I wanted to expand on what might be possible.

Initially, the system is being deployed in New York City, which has a large number of apartments that can be aggregated to create a meaningful amount of combined storage. Of course, the potential can be realized in areas outside of NYC. Every Electric is already looking to expand into the Boston area. There are several other areas where the existing system based on window air conditioners in urban areas could apply, particularly along the Northeast corridor. However, as the program evolves, there is tremendous potential outside of the region.

In Jackery’s home state of California, 4.5 TWh of solar and wind power was curtailed in the first half of 2026. More electricity wasted in six months in one state than many developing countries consume in an entire year. Residential solar often feeds into the grid when it just leads to curtailments elsewhere. Even with recent ESS installations, the Duck Curve is still deepening. If home batteries can help “decapitate the duck,” customers and the utility both stand to benefit. And all that renewable energy currently being wasted can contribute to reducing emissions.

In addition, every Jackery battery can operate as a solar generator, with a DC input. People can simply plug in a solar panel or two to recharge the battery. No additional inverter needed. As it does not send energy back into the grid, it also avoids the cost and complication of traditional grid-tied systems. Sending solar power directly to the battery would avoid having excess solar generation fed into the grid that contributes to overcapacity, deepening the back of the duck curve. Demand response could then come from a battery charged largely from solar, rather than from the grid. As the US is still trying to figure out balcony solar regulations, this would let people enjoy many of the benefits of small scale, plug-and-play solar now. No electricians needed.

Of course, when balcony solar regulations are figured out, Jackery has solutions like the SolarVault3 that I saw at IFA Berlin. Potentially, this system could integrate with point of use battery storage to form a kind of hybrid plug-in system. In the process, solar power could be utilized when it does the most for the grid and offers the greatest potential payback for the customer.

There is also a lot of potential for this concept to work outside of the US. In many parts of the world, most people live in apartments. As the planet warms, world cooling needs recently overtook heating for the first time. As the VPP is monitored via a Wi-Fi connection, there is no need to upgrade to a smart meter to take advantage of the capability. As the storage is already distributed, there is also reduced need to provide the grid upgrades to distribute the energy stored in a large battery during peak energy demand.

Currently, the plug-in VPP is only working with one model of battery in one application. That makes the deployment and scaling of the plug-in VPP easier. Large urban areas offer the clearest path to aggregate the storage needed for a plug-in VPP to work.

However, Jackery has sold over 8 million units over the past 15 years. This has established a level of customer trust and a reputation for reliability. Many of those portable power stations and solar generators can connect to Wi-Fi. That is potentially GWh of deployable storage.

In addition, portable power stations are very popular now. Many Jackery models are selling out. Preparedness is a major driver. Climate-related disasters amplify the fear and uncertainty. However, the batteries that people are buying to be prepared for emergencies offer far greater potential capability. The more batteries that are out there, the greater the potential.

As the software and capability of the VPP develop, multiple batteries have the potential to connect to amplify value, even if that capability isn’t being offered yet. Those batteries, in coordination with a VPP, could earn people money. Currently, the system is being run with one battery model, but Jackery offers a wide range of batteries with Wi-Fi connectivity that might be able to participate in the future. Once the program has proven itself with the initial application in urban areas, there is potential for expansion.

As people start earning money, even if they purchased the battery out of preparedness concerns, they would also start to see the broader benefit. When they gain both confidence and financial benefit from battery storage and renewable energy, they will tend to rationalize that benefit. That can lead to a broader shift in mindset. When you try to start with a rational argument, especially when that argument involves added costs, people are less likely to be supportive than if you start with the benefit.

Many of the pieces to make this happen have already existed. People have the ability to program Jackery batteries through the app to charge and discharge during certain parts of the day. This can work with TOU utility plans, where available. But it doesn’t take full advantage of the capabilities of a VPP. Customers do not get full credit for the storage capability. The true innovation comes from putting the pieces together, between the Jackery hardware, Every Electric software and the utility. If one of those pieces of the collaboration was missing, a plug-in VPP wouldn’t have the same potential.

One of the things that has impressed me about Jackery since I was first introduced to the company was their focus on solving customer problems. However, to create solutions, they also recognize that collaboration is often necessary. I already saw this with the flexible solar panels of SunBooster at IFA Berlin for balcony solar applications, and then again with Every Electric for plug-in VPP.

I asked Jackery founder and CEO Jack Sun about the value of collaboration in creating an environmentally friendly ecosystem:

This is also very important, because we have an open model, and we are more willing and more innovative…

We will actively collaborate with partners in areas such as power chips and more proactively in co-creation. We aim to accelerate the application of their innovations into our solar generator solutions. Our goal is to achieve faster and more efficient solar energy development through joint innovation. The generator can be installed in every household to help more families achieve energy independence more cost-effectively, while also reducing carbon emissions more quickly.

This focus on collaboration makes meaningful innovation happen. Just as I speculated here on the potential of plug-in VPP, other people will have their own ideas. Some will be different than this VPP concept. Many will be better ideas than the ideas I can come up with. Having innovative companies like Jackery and Every Electric actively seeking to work with partners creates potential for those ideas to come together. I look forward to seeing what new solutions come together next.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5675. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”
