#5674. FirstEnergy utilities get approval for $2.5B gas plant in West Virginia

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Consumer advocates and environmental groups opposed the project, citing concerns over load growth projections and shifting of project risk to ratepayers.

The project was prompted by an unnamed data center customer that would require an initial capacity of 100 MW in 2028, increasing incrementally to 1,012 MW by 2033, according to the commission staff.

A commission analysis found that Mon Power had projected a 2,997 MW summer peak in 2026. In 2028, the company could see a 60 MW capacity shortfall.

That grows to 2,194 MW in 2034 with the addition of new large loads, including data centers. That shortfall comes amid surging capacity prices in the PJM Interconnection has seen in recent years, the commission noted.

Groups like the Sierra Club argued that the utility’s $4 billion rate base would increase by 67%as a result of the project. Mon Power should create large load tariffs to ensure recovery from the customers prompting the electric infrastructure upgrades, the group added.

Anna Sommer, a principal at Energy Futures Group, testified on behalf of the West Virginia Citizen Action Group, Solar United Neighbors and Energy Efficient West Virginia, raising questions on the project’s levelized cost of energy.

“The underdevelopment of the Companies’ CC proposal introduces significant risk of cost increases and schedule delays,” Sommers wrote. The approved gas-fired power plant, called the Maidsville Energy Center, is expected to cost $2.48 billion.

The commission approved the construction surcharge, stating the mechanism had been used before – including for the canceled Mid Atlantic Power Pathway by Pepco Holdings, which ultimately was allowed to recover more than $80 million from ratepayers for the uncompleted project.

The surcharge mechanism would prevent any credit downgrading because of a lack of funds from operations flowing in, Mon Power had argued in its case.

In the “unlikely event that construction ceases or experiences extraordinary delays,” the commission will “initiate an appropriate proceeding to determine the applicable accounting and ratemaking treatment, surcharge or base rate recovery and the timing of such recovery,” the order says.

#5673. Groups Respond to Army’s Plan to Proceed with Lead Azide Detonation in Iowa

Army says it plans to detonate degraded lead in Iowa town no later than Nov. 24, despite court stay.

Des Moines, IOWA — The Iowa Environmental Council, Sierra Club, and Earthjustice will continue to advocate for accountability from the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) and the U.S. Army in the planned detonation of lead azide in Middletown, Iowa. Early Sunday morning, the groups filed declarations with the court that call out the Army’s inadequate public notice and information, and question the Army’s and EPA’s claims that all alternatives were given appropriate consideration.

The U.S. Department of Justice (DOJ) filed a statement with the court late on Saturday, Nov. 21, indicating that the Army intends to invoke emergency provisions of the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) to detonate 9,773.5 pounds of lead azide stored at the Iowa Army Ammunitions Plant, without a permit from the EPA. According to the DOJ’s court filings, the Army intends to do so no later than Nov. 24. On Nov. 20, the Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals granted Earthjustice’s request to put on hold a permit allowing the Army to detonate.

“People across numerous impacted communities speaking up and demanding accountability is the only reason there was an opportunity to pause and think about better ways to approach the lead azide problem,” said Earthjustice Attorney Thien Chau. “Yet despite claiming public safety is its top priority and public communication is essential to trust, the Army seemed more concerned over the weekend with finding a way to sidestep the judicial process than on meeting the public’s demands for greater transparency, notice to affected communities, and far stronger protection of people, property, and the environment from serious harm.”

This emergency is of the Army’s own making. The Army has not publicly provided details about why the lead azide was not disposed of before it became so dangerous. The Army’s own storage and inspection practices allowed a primary explosive to sit until it reportedly degraded to the point that any alternative disposal method is now deemed too dangerous. The lack of transparency raises real questions about the scope and extent of potential impacts.

“The new information from the DOJ indicates that the problem is significantly worse than the Army originally shared. It appears that detonation may be, at this point, the only feasible option due to how long this problem was allowed to persist. The public deserves to know immediately which communities will be impacted and how residents can most effectively protect themselves and mitigate impacts on their health and homes,” said Michael Schmidt, General Counsel for the Iowa Environmental Council.

The Army knew about this stockpile of dangerous munitions waste for years, and it knew since at least March that the material was becoming unstable. Yet, just one week ago, most Iowans — including those directly in the path of a toxic lead plume, the Iowa governor, and key state agencies and officials — were unaware the Army intended to detonate nearly five tons of lead azide.

The environmental groups argue the EPA and Army permitting documents have not demonstrated, nor provided appropriate documentation that all other safer alternatives to mitigate the material were thoroughly explored.

“The Army has clearly mishandled this situation, and Iowans and residents in our neighboring states deserve a full review of all of the missteps that led us to this point. However, if detonation is now the only option, it is imperative that everyone impacted has access to the full range of testing, mitigation, and remediation resources,” said Wally Taylor, Attorney and Legal Chair for the Sierra Club Iowa Chapter.

As of 11 a.m. CST on Sunday, Nov. 22, the Army has failed to provide the public with updated weather modeling and mapping showing the path of the lead dust plume, which means people impacted have little time to take precautions to protect their families and homes. The wind patterns have shifted significantly, which means that potential community exposures have likely changed without visible effort by the Army to provide updated information to the public.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5672. DTE Energy plans up to 150 megawatts of Michigan-based solar generation

DTE Energy recently announced plans to add up to 150 megawatts of new solar energy in Michigan, a move that will create construction jobs, support local communities, and save customers money on fuel costs, the company said.

In addition, because solar generation does not require fuel, the new projects will help reduce the company’s exposure to changing fuel prices over their operating lives. The projects will also support DTE’s work to provide customers with energy that is reliable, increasingly clean, and affordable.

The company stated that these projects are an investment in Michigan and its communities. By producing more Michigan-made energy that doesn’t require the purchase  of fuel, it can help lower energy costs for customers and reduce the reliance on fuel from outside the state. At the same time, these projects will create jobs and bring new investment and tax revenue into local communities, providing more resources for schools, first responders and the services Michigan families count on every day.

DTE is seeking competitive proposals for up to 150 megawatts of new Michigan-based solar generation. The procurement supports the requirements of the settlement agreement in Michigan Public Service Commission Case No. U-21662, approved May 15, 2025.

Projects must achieve commercial operation by June 30, 2030, be located in Michigan and connect to either the MISO transmission system or DTE’s distribution system. Interested bidders can register and obtain additional information through PowerAdvocate. Proposals are due Jan. 19, 2027, and DTE expects to execute contracts in the second or third quarter of 2027.

DTE is a producer of and investor in renewable energy in Michigan, generating enough clean energy from wind and solar to power nearly one million homes. As the company works toward net zero carbon emissions, it remains committed to growing its renewable energy portfolio while maintaining a balanced mix of energy resources that supports reliability and affordability for customers. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan builds on that commitment, calling for an additional 15 gigawatts of renewable energy by 2042.

#5670. Barcelona Is Suffering From The Hydrogen Transport Death Spiral

On November 21, Barcelona had at least 23 of its 46 hydrogen buses out of service because the plant supplying them had stopped producing fuel. Transports Metropolitans de Barcelona (TMB) reported a 2.5% to 3.8% reduction in scheduled bus service, with waits on some routes rising from seven to nine minutes. The buses were available to carry passengers. The hydrogen was not. ARA and La Vanguardia reported the disruption.

The problem runs through a single industrial fuel supply. TMB has a ten-year, €14.5 million hydrogen contract with Iberdrola. Alternative deliveries from Tarragona have fallen short, disrupted by industrial action and restrictions on hydrogen transport. The contract provides for compensation when fuel is not delivered. It cannot make up the missed service.

La Vanguardia connected the Barcelona stoppage with Cummins’ recommendation that customers stop using affected electrolysers following a safety review. Iberdrola cited a valve-system problem at the plant. Cummins’ November quarterly filing confirmed the September HyLYZER stop-use recommendation and said no formal recall had been initiated. TMB now has to assess the price of safely restarting fuel production and the reliability of the system it would be restarting.

Barcelona has not decided to retire the hydrogen buses. But the agency has a more important decision than repairing a plant: whether it should commit public money to keeping this fuel system in service for the remainder of the buses’ lives. Other European cities have faced that choice and walked away.

Aberdeen paid approximately £13.9 million for 25 hydrogen double-deckers that entered service in 2021. Hydrogen supply problems stranded the fleet in July 2024. After prolonged efforts to restore the operation, the city decided in February 2026 to dispose of the buses. A proposed sale would bring in £30,000 each for 23 buses going to First Bus and £63,000 each for two going to GeoPura. The total expected proceeds are £816,000, just 5.9% of the original vehicle expenditure. My September Aberdeen resale assessment examined the proposed terms and the conversion economics. First Bus has also considered battery-electric conversion.

Liverpool chose conversion. Ten of its 20 hydrogen buses had never carried passengers; the other ten had completed only 450 passenger-carrying journeys. In December 2025 the Liverpool City Region Combined Authority decided to convert all 20 to battery-electric operation, with the supplier covering the conversion costs. The buses had years of potential mechanical life, but the hydrogen supply arrangements had not delivered a usable transport service.

A hydrogen bus can become an uneconomic asset long before it wears out. Refuelling plants, fuel deliveries, maintenance specialists, software, fuel-cell modules and replacement parts have to remain available. As hydrogen fleets retire and new orders thin out, each remaining operator has fewer other customers sharing those support costs. Rising costs and uncertain service then encourage another operator to exit. That is the hydrogen transport death spiral. Battery-electric buses draw on a much larger manufacturing, charging and maintenance market.

The pressures already extend through the supply chain. Cummins has stopped seeking new commercial electrolyser business while customers contend with its HyLYZER stop-use recommendation. Quantron’s insolvency disrupted confidence in specialist vehicle support. Alstom acquired Cummins’ relevant rail fuel-cell operations to retain the staff and expertise needed to fulfil long-term contracts. Each case transfers more risk to organizations that have already bought hydrogen equipment.

New Flyer still sells hydrogen buses, but the North American order book is thin. Publicly identified hydrogen-bus orders numbered 288 in 2023, 96 in 2024 and no more than 45 gross in 2025, with about 15 in a stricter reconstruction. Another 38 buses appear in 2026 procurement decisions across differing approval stages. These are small volumes for a specialist vehicle line that needs engineering, spares and continuing product development.

Ballard is still developing bus fuel cells. Its November 5 Solaris announcement describes a new engine entering series production. Its GeoPura acquisition also shifts business attention toward integrated power systems and recurring energy-service revenue. My Ballard–New Flyer framework assessment found that the March agreement covering 500 modules is not an order for 500 buses. For a transit operator, the crucial test is a binding, affordable commitment to parts, diagnostics and maintenance throughout the vehicles’ remaining service lives.

TMB should compare the cost of safely restarting the hydrogen plant and running the buses with the cost of delivering the same passenger service through replacement vehicles. That includes the repair bill, future fuel contracts, station maintenance, spare parts, service interruptions, replacement buses, depot charging and any temporary vehicles required to keep routes operating. TMB already runs battery-electric buses and has local operating data to test a replacement plan.

A modest repair may justify running the existing hydrogen buses while alternatives are prepared. A major refurbishment, fresh fuel commitments and another decade of specialist support are a different investment. Aberdeen chose disposal. Liverpool chose conversion. Barcelona needs to decide what delivers dependable bus service at the lowest credible forward cost, without treating the original hydrogen spending as money it can recover.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines the Barcelona interruption, the Aberdeen and Liverpool exits, and the contracting support market. The paid section sets out how transit boards can compare restarting, bridging and retiring an existing hydrogen fleet.

#5669. Adani Cement to Deploy 410 Electric Trucks, Cement & Clinker

Ahmedabad, India — Adani Cement, part of the Adani Portfolio and one of India’s leading building materials solutions providers, flagged off Heavy-Duty Electric Trucks at its Sanghi Plant in Gujarat. This is the first-ever deployment of EV trucks by Adani Cement and a defining milestone in the company’s sustainability and green logistics journey.

Mr. Vinod Bahety, CEO of Cement Business, Adani Group, said, “The deployment at Sanghi is just the beginning. We have identified multiple routes across India for EV-based transport of clinker and cement. Every truck we electrify reduces our carbon footprint and insulates our logistics cost base from diesel price volatility. This is smart sustainability—it benefits the people, the planet, and the balance sheet. This is our commitment in action—to build a greener, more competitive cement company that serves India’s growth while protecting its future.”

The economic case for heavy-duty EV trucks in India’s cement sector has reached a structural tipping point. Over the past decade, diesel prices rose 60%, while Li-ion battery pack costs fell by approximately 70% and solar tariffs declined 47%.

Diesel trucks emit approximately 0.7375 kg CO₂ per km and when powered by renewable energy, EV trucks can eliminate up to 100% of these freight emissions, making logistics electrification a powerful lever for Scope 3 decarbonization. Adani Cement’s 410-truck fleet is projected to reduce over 25,000 tonnes of CO₂ annually, displacing the equivalent of 11 million litres of diesel per year, with plans to expand across additional routes and plants.

India is the world’s second-largest cement producer, and the movement of limestone, clinker, and finished cement across vast supply chains is an essential, but carbon-intensive, part of the sector’s operations. Adani Cement’s EV fleet deployment addresses both the cost and climate dimensions of this challenge directly.

#5667. COP31 Plants A Big Wet Kiss On Failed Carbon Capture Technology

COP31, the annual UN climate conference that took place this month, ended on a sour note after negotiators were unable to reach an agreement to include language calling for a transition away from fossil fuels in the final communiqué  — language that was included two years ago at the conclusion of COP 29 in Dubai.

How is it possible to explain such backsliding at a time when a Kelvin wave — a wall of unusually hot water — is impacting the coast of every country along the eastern edge of the Pacific Ocean? Authorities say unusually high temperatures all across Europe led to the premature death of more than 50,000 people last summer. In addition, huge swaths of the Earth’s land mass are experiencing either severe drought or punishing floods. How can anybody ignore the events happening before their very eyes?

You know the answer — MONEY! According to Kathryn McCallum, the director of strategy at the Climate Action Network Australia, there were 624 delegates from the 14 Pacificc Small Island Developing States at the conference in Turkey this month and 700 paid lobbyists for the carbon capture industry. In an email to CleanTechnica, she said,

“There is no public momentum from parties in the COP31 process asking for carbon capture, utilization and storage, yet it is in the final draft text. As a result, in the coming hours, parties at COP31 will decide whether to lock in a [methane] fired future with a rapid scale-up of carbon dumping. The potential inclusion of a huge increase in global carbon capture, utilization and storage (CCUS) would be one of the biggest loopholes for the fossil fuel industry ever to be included in a COP text. Climate advocates are highly alarmed and are urging parties to come to their senses and make decisions that advance global climate action rather than the interests of the gas industry.”

As it turns out, the knife in the back delivered by fossil fuel interests at COP31 was too much for negotiators from the EU and the UK to swallow. According to reNEWS, the European Union expressed its “profound disappointment” with the draft cover decision following the close of the COP31 conference.

“At a point where the world urgently needs action to avert disastrous level of global warming, the draft cover decision falls short,” a joint EU statement read. “We will not endorse a cover decision that does not respond to the science, that disregards the implementation gap, and risks deepening the chasm between global commitments and the action required to correct the overshoot and bring 1.5°C within reach. We will not endorse a cover decision that does not even mention fossil fuels, the driver of climate change.”

The statement listed a number on other concerns with the draft cover decision, including that the draft extols carbon capture, usage, and storage technologies “disproportionally” as a method to deal with the climate crisis. This is magical thinking, the statement by the EU said.

The UK also refused to be sucked in by the absurd claims that carbon capture was a plausible alternative to reducing the world’s reliance on fossil fuels. The Independent reports that UK energy secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh expressed regret that the talks had failed to produce the “bold ambition” needed, as the negotiations became mired in disagreement over a draft agreement that contained no mention of fossil fuels.

The UK formally objected to a proposed deal that “moves us backwards on the science” of tackling climate change. In a statement, Fahnbulleh said, “This COP sought to confront hard truths — that global warming is set to overshoot 1.5C and that we must act with ever more urgency to address it. The moment required bold ambition and we have been working tirelessly to try to build consensus. It is immensely disappointing we have been unable to do so.”

There are a number of ways to remediate the emissions from burning fossil fuels, which are nothing more than stored sunshine from millions of years ago. We could irradiate them with unicorn farts, incinerate them in nuclear fusion facilities, are store them in the basement at Mar-A-Loco, where large quantities of toxic fumes are already present. There is only one problem — none of those solutions work. In fact, the only thing that does work is burning less fossil fuels, until we are able to phase them out entirely.

In  2025, Mark Jacobson, a professor of civil and environmental engineering at Stanford University, and his colleagues, published a research paper in the journal Environmental Science and Technology.  In it he wrote that if the nations of the world transitioned to renewable energy — solar, wind, and hydroelectric power — they would eliminate energy-related air pollution deaths and CO2-equivalent emissions while reducing their energy needs by 54.4 percent, annual energy costs by 59.6 percent, and annual social costs — energy, plus health, plus climate — by 91.8 percent. Who wouldn’t want that?

On the other hand, Jacobson and his team wrote that every dollar spent on carbon capture instead of wind, water, and solar, “increases carbon dioxide pollution, energy requirements, energy costs, pipelines, and total social costs.” This holds true even if zero-emission energy systems power the technology deployed to extract carbon dioxide. “It’s always an opportunity cost to use clean, renewable energy for direct air capture instead of replacing a fossil fuel CO2 source, just like it’s an opportunity cost to use it for AI or bitcoin mining. You’re preventing renewables from replacing fossil fuel sources because you’re creating more demand for those renewables,” he said.

“Opportunity costs” is the operative concept here. If you are in Peoria, you cannot be in Philadelphia. If you spend $10.00 on pretzels, you cannot spend it on vegetables. If you buy a new car, you cannot use that money to go to Cabo San Lucas. The money the world pisses away on carbon capture cannot be used to build solar, wind, and hydro resources. It’s as simple as that.

The take away here is that fossil fuels will always win the policy debate. So what will drive the transition to renewable energy? Lower prices for renewables and eliminating concerns about energy insecurity from geopolitical disruptions like the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. Low prices always win and people are pretty sick and tired of paying six bucks for diesel while watching the cost of electricity soar higher and higher.

“The first one now shall later be last,” Bob Dylan taught us. That’s a message of hope inside the carbon dioxide cloud the fossil fuel industry has gifted us. The only question is whether the transition will happen soon enough to avoid a climate catastrophe. It’s hard to imagine destroying a planet and decimating your customer base is a good long term business strategy, but that’s what the executives in charge of the world’s oil, methane, and coal industries seem to think makes for good business. There are none so blind as those who will not see.

Steve writes about the interface between technology and sustainability from his home in Florida or anywhere else The Force may lead him. He is proud to be "woke" and believes weak leaders push others down while strong leaders lift others up. You can follow him on Substack at https://stevehanley.substack.com/ but not on Fakebook or any social media platforms controlled by narcissistic yahoos.

#5666. Thornova Solar and Nextpower strengthen strategic cooperation

Solar module manufacturer Thornova Solar and Nextpower, a provider of advanced solar tracker and structural solutions, marked a milestone in their growing strategic cooperation with the signing of a Master Supply Agreement (MSA).

The agreement, announced at RE+ 2026, further strengthens the collaboration between the two companies, combining Thornova Solar’s U.S.-made solar modules with Nextpower’s U.S.-manufactured steel frame technology. The expanded effort is designed to deliver increased domestic content opportunities and enhanced structural performance, providing greater value to customers developing utility-scale and commercial and industrial (C&I) solar projects across the United States.

According to the companies, integrating advanced steel frame solutions into selected Thornova Solar module series addresses two increasingly important priorities in the U.S. solar market: maximizing domestic content and improving module resilience under demanding environmental conditions.

The combined solution offers several key benefits:

The solution is particularly relevant for projects in demanding U.S. environments, including high-wind regions across the Midwest and Southeast, where structural reliability and long-term module performance are critical considerations.

“The signing of this Master Supply Agreement represents an important step in strengthening our relationship with Nextpower and expanding the value we deliver to U.S. customers,” said Andrea Bodenhagen, global marketing director of Thornova Solar. “By bringing together U.S.-made solar modules and domestically manufactured steel frame technology, we are helping our customers increase domestic content while benefiting from stronger, more resilient module solutions. This is exactly the kind of collaboration needed to advance a competitive U.S. solar manufacturing ecosystem.”

“We’re excited to deepen our strategic collaboration with Thornova and bring modules featuring Nextpower’s domestically manufactured steel frames to the U.S. market,” said Jenya Meydbray, vice president and general manager of Nextpower. “NX CoreFrame offers a significant opportunity to advance module strength and reliability, addressing industry challenges such as glass breakage and demanding environmental loads while delivering greater confidence to solar developers and owners.”

The MSA reflects the shared commitment of both companies to strengthening domestic manufacturing and delivering solutions that respond to the evolving technical and commercial requirements of the U.S. solar market.

#5665. The European Competitiveness Fund, the Backbone of a Modern European Investment Policy

European businesses call for a robust, well-funded European Competitiveness Fund

Dear Chancellors, Prime Ministers, Presidents,

Europe is losing ground in technological leadership and struggles to scale strategic technologies, while volatile energy costs and external dependencies continue to dampen business confidence.

Europe’s path to enduring technological leadership relies on our ability to scale strategic technologies. By stabilising energy costs, providing a path to clean industrial renewal and cultivating European resilience, we are laying the groundwork for robust business and investors’ confidence. Public investment has a crucial role to play to address these vital challenges. We require a smarter, more strategic investment policy for Europe.

We, more than 75 undersigned businesses, call on European leaders to agree to a Multiannual Financial Framework (MFF) commensurate with Europe’s challenges, to protect its investment capacity and place long-term productivity growth, resilience and decarbonisation at its core. We believe the European Competitiveness Fund (ECF) represents a step-change in the EU’s investment policy and must be strengthened in the remaining negotiations.

The ECF with its toolbox projects the ambition to provide tailored support to the investment and scale-up challenge Europe faces. This is a positive evolution of the EU funding landscape. With a bigger role for the EU’s derisking instrument InvestEU, the ECF can become Europe’s modern investment vehicle with an increased use of guarantees, loans and equity to unlock strategic investments.

At the same time, non-repayable support needs to be dedicated to scaling up sectors that deliver high strategic value but cannot yet secure commercial capital in Europe. Ramp-up support for Europe’s cleantech manufacturing, including support for OpEx, is needed to make Europe’s bid to regain technological leadership credible.

The advisory services for SMEs and the single access portal promises a simpler access to funding for firms across Europe. If successful, the ECF can pool additional national funds through merit-based procedures open to eligible projects across the single market to bring further alignment and synergies into European industrial development.

Europe’s energy import dependency has become a strategic vulnerability for European businesses. To increase our economic resilience, the EU must prioritise investments into energy sovereignty. Incentives need to pivot capital allocation away from fossil fuel infrastructure, and towards clean energy and electrification. From 2028, the next MFF should not support new fossil-fuel infrastructure that prolongs dependency on fossil fuels, while providing narrowly defined safeguards for security of supply. We need steering mechanisms to reduce fossil fuel dependencies. EU funding can help to tackle high upfront costs and derisk the switch to cleaner and more reliable energy. Otherwise, long investment cycles create severe fossil fuel lock-in effects that threaten our operational autonomy with increasingly volatile fossil energy prices.

Europe’s investment gap cannot be solved by a smarter, more strategic funding approach alone. The confidence into European competitiveness needs to translate into a sizable budget for the clean transition to have lasting impact. The Commission proposes an average annual budget of €3.3 billion for a clean transition and industrial decarbonisation window of the ECF. This number is dwarfed by recurring estimates of the annual climate investment deficit of €344 billion. At the same time, according to the OECD, industrial subsidies have reached the highest levels since the financial crisis 2008.

We urge European leaders to respond to the evolving investment environment and adopt the European Parliament’s proposal to increase the size of the ECF by €26.6 billion and to allocate a substantial share of the additional funding for industrial decarbonisation and clean transition. The more efficient use of financial instruments and a strategic prioritisation can improve the impact of EU funding but cannot substitute the investment needs Europe is facing to boost competitiveness and security.

The next long-term budget and the European Competitiveness Fund must become the investment trigger for European businesses to regain competitiveness at the global scale, based on clear incentives and regulatory certainty. We businesses urge leaders to demonstrate a long-term vision and make the ECF the cornerstone of a new EU green industrial strategy.

Download the letter to see the full list of signatories.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5664. Bright Horizon utility lands Microsoft data center deals amid Wyoming buildout

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Cheyenne Light — with a 439-MW peak load record — is working on a 3-GW near-term data center pipeline, with an additional 2.5 GW in emerging opportunities, per company officials.

Cheyenne Light, Fuel and Power Co. has entered into agreements to build distribution and transmission infrastructure to serve three Microsoft data centers planned for Wyoming, the company said Tuesday in a filing at the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.

The redacted agreements require Microsoft to pay for the infrastructure through “contribution in aid of construction” payments, according to the filing.

Cheyenne Light — a subsidiary of Bright Horizon Energy Corp., a utility holding company created by the Nov. 13 merger of Black Hills Corp. and NorthWestern Energy Group — doesn’t believe FERC has jurisdiction over the retail agreements but filed them for review “out of an abundance of caution” due to the agency’s recent statements related to cost recovery agreements for transmission costs caused by large load customers.

The Microsoft agreements reflect a massive data center buildout that is underway in southeast Wyoming where Cheyenne Light operates.

The utility is executing on a 3-GW data center pipeline, including 600 MW of Microsoft and Meta projects, Black Hills officials said during a Nov. 5 earnings conference call. It is also in initial talks on an additional 2.5 GW of early-stage data center opportunities.

Cheyenne Light has about 46,000 retail electric customers in the Cheyenne area and set a peak load record of 439 MW in July, according to Black Hills’ most recent U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission earnings report.

The utility in September agreed to supply a Google data center project in Cheyenne with 590 MW and manage 2.1 GW of third-party power supply in a microgrid, Black Hills said in its earnings presentation.

The agreements with Google contain cost pass-through mechanisms that protect customers from costs related to the planned data center while protecting utility returns from inflation, interest rate volatility and other cost pressures, Black Hills noted in the SEC filing.

Cheyenne Light intends to serve the data centers through its Large Power Contract Service tariff and a proposed Large Customer Transmission Cost Adjustment Mechanism, which are designed to insulate the utility’s customers from the infrastructure costs related to data centers. Black Hills expects the Wyoming Public Service Commission will make a decision on the transmission tariff proposal by mid-2027.

Cheyenne Light plans to use the transmission tariff to recover the costs of two recently proposed transmission projects totaling $686 million needed to serve large loads, including the Google data center. Under the tariff, large load customers would pay their share of the transmission expansion.

The Google data center is set to begin operating late next year and ramp up to its full load by the end of 2030, according to the SEC filing. To serve it, Cheyenne Light plans to build a $1.8 billion, utility-owned 564-MW gas-fired power plant between 2027 and 2029, through an unregulated affiliate. The company expects the deal with Google will boost its net income by $150 million a year starting in 2030.

In its SEC filing, Black Hills warned that the Google deal increases its risk by making it more reliant on one customer.

“Our agreements with Google represent a significant long-term growth opportunity and are expected to contribute meaningfully to future revenues, earnings, cash flows and capital investment,” the company said. “As a result, we will have increased exposure to a single large customer and to trends affecting the data center, cloud computing and artificial intelligence industries.”

Also, Black Hills said that large load demand forecasts face “significant uncertainty” and could be affected by technological advancements, economic conditions and regulatory developments.

“Although the agreements contain customer credit support, termination payment and other investment recovery provisions, those protections may not fully mitigate the impact of reduced customer demand, contract disputes, non-renewal or customer default,” Black Hills said.

#5663. Solar Industry Supports BAAJA While Environmental Orgs Oppose It

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) is an interesting piece of legislation. It is getting a ton of support from the solar sector, but it is heavily opposed by environmental organizations.

Following the passage of BAAJA in the US Senate the other day, Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), stated the following: “America is now one step closer to lower energy costs, more clean energy and a more reliable electric grid. Senators Lee, Capito, Heinrich, and Whitehouse deserve tremendous credit for advancing this bipartisan bill.”

The organization adds some more detail and why it thinks this is such a good piece of legislation: “SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.”

That sounds very appealing, doesn’t it? Why wouldn’t we want that?

Well, the Sierra Club argues the bill goes in quite the opposite direction. The storied organization states the following: “While a rogue president is waging a war on clean energy, the Senate rushed a deeply flawed bill that furthers the buildout of fossil fuels, strips away power from communities and Tribes, threatens our clean water and public lands, and endangers wildlife. The House of Representatives has an equal voice in the legislative process. They must reject the Senate’s fast-tracked bill and demand debate and consideration to deliver for the people, not corporate polluters and Donald Trump. The American people made a clear call merely weeks ago: our elected leaders must address soaring costs and a corrupt system that prioritizes corporate polluter profits over our health, our wallets, and our future.”

It seems the issue is that BAAJA would make it much easier to put up power plants, skipping all kinds of environmental protections. That is why Republicans support it in the first place — they are always keen to plow through environmental protections. The reason SEIA supports it is clear enough — the organization is just thinking about solar power plants and looking at the potential for more growth in the industry, especially considering the fact that solar is now typically the cheapest option for new power capacity and has been dominating new installations.

So, the question is: would BAAJA really disproportionately support solar power, or would it lead to a lot more fossil fuel power plants and an edge to the old, dirty power technologies? Can the legislation be both good and bad, or is someone getting this wrong?
